PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

NOTHING strengthens a woman's self-oonfidence like a proposal. NAPOLEON made a great many men prominent—. among others, the Duke of Wellington. Music may be considered classical when it corn. man& praise from people whom it bores. SATIRE is like a mustard-plaster; we don't feel the sting until we apply it to ourselves. MANY a man ruins himself trying to live up to his reputation.. Willow a woman takes you into her confidence, don't allow yourself to be too much taken in. IF you want to mislead people as to your real character, be perfectly honest with them.

" Grvz us a pen'orth o' oysters gov'nor." "Why not go tuppence, and have a whole one?" MANY a young man who has entered on a carter has been glad soon afterwards to get a steady job. IF some people were brighter, they wouldireflect more; and, by the way, if they would reflect more, they might be brighter. A MAN is startled sometimes when he thinks of his former ignorance; but he generally feels that his present knowledge is ample IT is hnpossible to satisfy everybody ; and the folks who endeavour to avoid satisfying anybody have almost as difficult a task.

" REGOIE Lummox is such a conceited thing," said Molly. "He calls me a man-hater just because he heard I said I didn't iike him."

WHEN some people are building up a reputation they are inclined to use inferior material in places where they think it won't show. COMMENT.—Maud : " Cholly hasn't been quite himself of late." Rose: "No! I hadn't noticed any improvement." AN INDICATION.—She: "Has he distinguished himself in any way?" He: "1 judge from his distingue air that he has not." "No," said the Klondike miner,- gently but firmly, to the applicant for relief: "I'll give you money, but I can't let you have any food." The applicant for relief turned sorrowfully away. BROWN (after shopping) : "Mrs. Smith manages to get such bargains, and SO many of them !" Brown: "Oh, well, I suppose money is no object with her." DIVERSE METHODS.—" Every man has an ideal woman that he never mentions to his wife." " Yes ; and every woman has an ideal man that she is everlastixgty holding up to her husband." LADY (to applicant for servant's place) : "Have you ever been charged at the police-court with any crime ?"—Applicant (promptly): "No mum; but 'a —blushing—" I'm being courted by a police. man."

BEFORE AND Arm—" Before we were married you used to write me three letters a day." "Did I, really?" "Yes, you did. And now you are annoyed just because I ask you to write me a little bit of a cheque." SLEEP.—The villain clutched the jagged stone frantically. " With this," he hissed, his bosom heaving, "I could pat him to sleep for ever r' For -there are, to a virtual certainly, sermons in stones.

HAROLD FREDERICK says the day will yet come when Paris will repent her hasty condemnation of M. Zula, and name a street for him. We suppose what Mr. Frederick really means to say is that Paris will rue it.

Bob Hard. up cheerfully) : " Yes—l know; but I'd ba willing to let her do most of the talking." "Oh, Mr. Cynicus," said Miss Daisy Candor, with the ready sympathy of sweet sixteen, "why, I'm sure all your friends would bury you gladly." Host (confidentially): "To tell you the truth, Robbins, I am. I never invite anybody here that a sane woman could pos- sibly take the least fancy to." TARING No UNNECESSARY RlSKS.—Scene, pit of theatre. Second Theatre-goer (whispering back) : "Ask her yourself, please. You don't know her as well as I do." _ _ _

A BOY being asked to describe a kitten, said: "A kitten is remarkable for rushing like mad at nothing whatever and stopping before it gets there." It must have been the same boy who thus defined scandal—" It is when nobody ain't done nothing, and somebody goes and tells."

"Nothing, nothing," she replied, trying to blush, "only those are my senti- ments." ' SIR HERCULES LANGRISHE was a wonder even in the days of four-bottle men. You can put the old poets up on a shelf in the library where they cao't force themselves on your attention, and let them stay there." LADIES' LETTER.,

ah DEAR NELLIE,

There are many pretty styles in summer finery for the young ones. Foulards in small patterns make charming best dresses for girls over nine years old, and a nice way of making up a blue and white one, for instance, is with a gathered skirt edged with a band of cream lace insertion laid on the foulard, and a full, pouched bodice, the fronts of which are striped with three perpendicular bands of the lace: These latter start from the edge of a deep, slightly-pointed yoke, made of alternate bands of tucked mull-muslin and lace insertion, a small falling collar of •lace-bordered foulard outlining the yoke. The sleeves are finished with lace, and the sash, which is tied at the left side of the front, is of wide blue satin- ribbon. The same style would look very well carried out in deeper blue foulard with trimmings of ecru lace, the yoke in this case being of piece lace laid over white satin or glace silk. Another pretty model would be successful in foulard or nun's veiling, the collar being either white or of the colour of the material, and the small pointed vest of tucked muslin or fine lawn.

A smart fichu is a never-failing help where av old bodice requires smartening up or otherwise altering, and it gives a becoming breadth to toc narrow shoulders. But a fichu needs very careful adjustment, else it becomes a thing of beauty spoilt. A well-fitting fichu must be caretully shaped at the neck, and given sufficient spring on the shoulders, or it will bunch up and give the wearer a hump-backed appearance. A good recipe for a slightly-fluted fichu is to cut out a half-circle, in diameter about a yard, and out of this take an oval-shaped piece from the centre, leaning thE fichu about seven or eight inches wide at thE centre of the back, and sloping away to a poini at either end. This secures a well-fitting and simple fichu.

Simplicity is observed as an element of novelty exhibited in the latest millinery ; indeed, the multi- brimmed hats, with their artistic lines, are often not adorned at all. One hat I saw the other day was a yellow straw chapeau, wreathed with pink roses resting on a background of black chiffon ; a combination of colour that made for coolness of effect, an absence of effort, and undoubted style. A lady whom I met the other day was wearing a hat with black velvet round it, all set in groups of threes. The hat, made of cream-coloured chiffon, with a crown of black fancy straw, had for trim- ming a large rosette of black velvet ribbon, and a bunch of velvet carrots, realistically orange, with a circle of green round the top of each, and a spray or two of the vegetable's feathery foliage. Green currants are rather a favourite trimming, and among flowers the rose is a long way ahead of any other for purposes of millinery.

I have of late observed a numbet of silk dresses worn by ladies. A dress of white glace silk trimmed with frills, each one bordered with narrow black velvet, is being made with elbow sleeves, also finished with frills and a tucked muslin chemisette run with the narrow black velvet. Another white glace silk is embroidered all over with black dots, aid is trimmed with an embroidery in which black and white are about equally mingled. Many ladies will wear dark blue skirts, pale blue anC, white striped shirts, with knots of scarlet, scarlet sashes, khaki felt hats, and shoes to match.

Straw paillettes are an innovation, used chiefly for day gowns, in meandering patterns, rather than massed solidly together. Gold braiding and gold ria,lon is a feature on white piqué, linen or sergedresses, forming the neckbands, and worked in -handsome patterns on the revers and lapels. Drill, linen, and nankeen are materials preferable for washolg dresses to pique, which is apt to pre- sent such a very gtarched and stiff aspect after many visits to the laundry. A very nice dress, worn by a la dy I saw yesterday, was in white erpe de Chine, inlet kal over with motifs of black lace, and a deep band of black chiffon round :,rie waist. The best ' fashionable dresses show evidence of an amount of handwork which demonstrates the fact that the high charges of good dressmakers are accounted for by the time and labour spent on clothes rather than by the material.

Your loving JENNIE.

THE MOST NUTRITIOUS.

14at 111111 P S 3 s

GRATEFUL-COMFORTING.

.

aREAKFAST AND SUPPER.

"WOMEN'S CHAT.'

The Queen, who has been entertaining a large num- ber of guests during her residence at Windsor Castle, will leave for Osborne about the middle of the month f.r her annual summer stay in the Isle of Wight. Her Majesty intends to follow the usual plans for the year, and wits arrive at Balmoral by the end of August, where the Court will stay until the middle of Nevember. If all goes well, the Queen will entertain the Shah of Persia at Osborne. He will doubtless be invested with the Order of the Garter, a decoration which was conferred upon the late Shah when he went to Windsor Cas:le in 1873, during his first visit to Europe. The Queen was a purchaser at the Prince of Wales' live stock sale at Sandringham the other day. The taste for farming is inherited by our Royal Family. George 111. was affectionately known to many of his subjects as " Farmer George," and the late Prince Consort had an enthusiastic interest in agricultural pursuits. The Royal herd and flock at Sandringham were established in 1863. They represent some of the best stock in the country, and the Prince has, of course, taken many prizes at all the principal shows. The Royal farms at Windsor were greatly extended by the Prince Consort, and the Queen has always seen that they were most carefully and liberally maintained. When her Maj-sty is at the Castle she frequently pays visits to the cufferent farms, and it is said that she knows quite as much about her poultry, as about the horses and cattle, and the general management of the farms.

The scmmer shows in connection with the Ladies' Kennel Association was greatly extended this year, and nearly everyone, except a few who had failed to gain expected prizes voted it an immense success. The National Cat Show,the Ladies' Poultry Club,the Ladies' Cage Bird Club. all joined forces, making a huge show of doge, cats canaries, parrots, pigeons, and fowls. The fashionable classes were largely represented by aristocra- tic women, and still more aristocratic doge and pets. The Princess of Walei toek several prizes, including the five hundred guinea Dholpure Cup awarded to Chain. pion "Alex " winner of over fifty first prizes. Every class of fanciers' pees was seen at its best, and it was amusing to listen to the different termsof endear- ment bestowed upon them a; they pissed before the judges. The cats were led round by their fair owners, some turning sulky, and refusing to be coaxed along. It was decided to give a genial prize for deporment, which was given to the cat of most grace and dignity without regard to physical beauties. It is of curse rather a delicate office to be a judge at a Ladies' Show, but, though it is perhaps not wholly free from danger, still women are much more sportsmanlike than they used to he, and take a beating more philoaophicelly. The morganatic marriage of the Archduke Franz Ferdinand ,heir to the Austrian threne,with the Countess Sophie Cholek, is ia some respects unique. Many Pfinces have made a clandestine marriage, but this is nothing of the kind. Never before has the heir to a throne openly contracted a morganatic union, with tbe full consent of the reigning Sovereign. The Archduke instead of renouncing the succession, has taken an oath that he accepts all the consequences of his marriage,andithoueh he may one day be Emperor, his wife can never be Empress. The result cab hardly be an entirely happy erre, Ewing what this distiection must mean in a multitude of ways in all the ceremonies and precedents of one of the proudest Courts of Europe. Teough the Countess Sophie, who has now been given the title of Princess Hohenberg, is not of Royal birth she belongs to an ancient and noble family. It is therefore a pity that the more simple way of raising her to Royal rank could not have been adop:ed, in order to remove all obstacles to her marriage with the Archduke. Madame Sarah Grand recently gave an interesting lecture upon the" miseries that need not be." and surely this affair, which in spite of all must necessarily put a good deal of womanly suffering upon the Countess, is an apt illustra- tion of needlessly creating troubles. The time has come when much of the nonsense about Royal blood should be relegated to the dark ages. Ic is owing to this silly tradition that there is scarcely a throne in Europe wbore sovereign is entirely of the same nationality as that of the people. It would et least appear to be more important to the people of Austria and Hungary that ' their Empress should be one of themselves rather than some fereien Princess who was a stranger to their national character- istics. This would FOOM to be the view that is taken in those two countries of the marriage of the Arehduke. There is perhaps an element of romance in it, which appeals to the popular fancy, hut the general impression is one of delight and appreval that the Archduke has had the courage and determination to marry the woman of his choice.

Canon Gore, addressing a meting on the subject, of lead poisoning in the potteries,observei that the remedy was an easy one, and if ladies could do nothing more, they could make a fuss about the sale of lead-glazed china, and so produce an effect on the trade. It is to he feared however, that the women and girls conc.rned are in a bad position. if they depend upon this succour for any relief of which they may be in need. In the first place, the vast majority of ladies have not the remotest idea what lead-glazing is, and in the second it is impes- eible to forget that for years and years, the Press has been appealing almost in vain, to our sex, not to wear the plumage of beautiful birds. The widow's cap of the moment is a very dainty little arrangement, and strongly in contrast with the very serinus piece of headgear donned by widows of a couple of decades back. They hid nearly all the hair, and could certainly never have been considered either useful or ornamental. In fact its raison (rare seemed to have been the expression on the part of the wearer, of utter indifference as to her good looks, or the opinion of any- one: The now popular little Marie Stuart colic, with long ends at the back is a very much better idea from every point of view. The summer sales will soon be in full swing, indeed a few leading houses have already commenced operations. Shopkeepers have evidently become frightened at the long spell of cold rainy weather, and seem bent ora getting rid of—at any price—their stook laid up for the hot days. To the woman who has refrained from filling her wardrobe with summer attire, until more assured of the weather, this season's sales will prove d boon. She will be able to get all she requires, if she goes carefully about it,at less than half what she would have had to pays month or six weeks ago," and the saving effected, will reconcile her ahould she get little wear out of her purchases. Costumes selected by the none too well dowered woman, at this period, are usually chosen with due fore- thought!to the summer holiday,and aripbthar this is to be spent inland, or by the sea,smart little gowns which are effective and yet not ton elaborate, or expensive, are eminently getirahle. A deep blue, rather darker than royal, and lighter than navy is a capital colour to select. Trimmed with white it is suitable wear until late autumn is tvith 119, and if this is then removed and velvet, braid, or other seasonable trimming substituted, the gown will be wearable throughout the winter with a warm slip oodice an.] a heavier petticoat. HOW TO SHOOT STRAIGHT.

Are you Right or Left-Eyed?

Tte autTct of the fitting of a gun or rife to the shoulder is dealt with in a letter communicated to the " Field " recently. But, says the correspondent, "there is another imporcant matter to be considered, namely, the question of eyesight. A great many persons at the present time would be unable to answer the question, Are you right or left eyed 2. or in other words,' Which of your two eyes have the stronger power of vision?' Yet the fact remains that in every mars either the right or left eye is s'ronger than its companion, and therefore unconscious of the fact as he may be, every man locates an objtmt in that particular spot in which his stronger eye tells him that it is. "A simple test can la's employed to discover which is the governing eye. All that ynir have to do is, first of all, with both eyes open, look through a small ring, held bfAween the finger and thumb at a distance at about 18 in. from the face, at any small and clearly defined object, such as a nail in the wall. When the object apoears exactly in the centre of the ring shut your right eye, being careful not to move the ring fri,m its position and if the object is still to be seen thirough the ring, you may be sure that your left eye, that istthe only one now in use, is the stronger of the two. A Good Test.

"If, on the contrary, you find that the object has apparently moved and is outside the ring, try your right eye open and your left eye shut, when you will find that the object has returned to its proper place in the centre of the ring, therefore demonstrating that your right eye is the master eye, or in other words, that even when you bac both eyes open, your right eye, being so much the stronger, placed the object in the centre of the ring. although at the time you were ignorant as to which of your eyes was doing tho work. "It must not be concluded, however, from the fore- glinz remarks that when using a shot gun it is desir- able to shut an eyo . very far from it, and for this reason, that if your right eye is the master eye, then the mere fact of your left eye being open cannot interfere with the work of your right, but, whereas your right eye is pointing out to you the actual position of the object aitutd at, your left eye, althlugh not helping you at all in this respect, adds very materially to the length and compass of your vieon, and also aids you in defin- ing wi.h grear,er clearness the outline of the object.

Should We Shut One Eye When Shooting.

"Supposing that we adinit (oi- the sake of argument that a man whose lei t eye is the master eye has double the eaing ' power in that eve than ha hai in the other, it will be readily understood that by shutting his left (17e ho deprives himself of two third, of his available power of vision, and yet if he be a left eyed man, and is in the habit of shooting or clesires t) shoot from his right shoulder, how is he to shoot straight without shutting his left eye ? • ' "There are two ways out of the difficulty ; one is to learn to shoat from the left shoulder, an aceomplish- ment that is easily acquired by smne men, but to the major,ty presents such a feeling of awkwardness and restraint that unlass t,ha knack is acquire.l alter a few days' practice it is doubtful whether the game is worth the candle. The other plan reerred is to have what is termed a cross-eyed gun ; that is, a gun with au abnor- mal amount of .cast which will vary in extent according to the degree of superimrity which the left eye has over the right, and also according to the build of the shooter. _ _

(Copyright.) THE TEMPTATION OF DUNCAN RUTLAND.

By JOSEPH HOCKING,

Author' of "The Birthright," "Mistress Nancy Molesworth," "All Men are Liars," "The Story of Andrew Fairfax," &c.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Alizon Neville, who belongs to an old Roman Catholic family, pays, for curiosity's sake, a secret visit to Tudor Chapel, the Nonconformist place of worship at Lynford. She hears the new minister, Duncan Rutland, and by chance is thrown across his path. The virility of his Protestantism makes her half dislike him. She fears he will do much harm among the Catholic population in unsettling their beliefs. Events so arrange themselves that at her instigation Father Sheen, her confessor, agrees to meet Duncan for a public debate about Roman Catholicism. In the first debate 'Rutland easily disposes of his Catholic antagonist. Much chagrined, Alizon un- wittingly encourages some Catholic weavers to trap the young minister, so that he shall not appear at the second debate.

CHAPTER, VII.

THE SCENE AT EOGGART'S CLOUGH.

Presently Alizon Neville made up her mind. Catholic or no Catholic, it was her duty to warn Duncan Rutland. But how? She could not tell! anyone to do it, for by so doing she would revea the plot of her co-religionists. Much as she hated the action she did not wish the poor fellows to be punished. They thought they were doing what would be both pleasing to her and to Father Sheen. Besides, she shrank from letting anyone know that she had any knowledge of the matter. What, then, could she do? She remembered Mrs. Pickup's words: "00'11 be on his way theer by naa. ' But perhaps he might not be. She knew where Duncan lodged. She would go and warn him. It was a hard thing to do, but it was her duty, nevertheless. If he had left the house she must follow him to Boggart's Clough. He had accompanied her there once, now she would repay his services tenfold. But she hoped, profoundly hoped, that he would not have left his lodgings. Dinner was to be early at the Priory that night, so that she might accompany her mother to the debate. She knew, too, that she ought to go home at once; but, instead, she made straight for the road in which Duncan lodged. It was dark, but she recognised the house. It had been pointed out to her on one occasion. When she knocked at the door Mrs. Nutter appeared. "Is Mr. Rutland in ? " "Noa," said the woman, - peering at her curiously. "Can you tell me where he is ? " she asked, with a heavy heart. She dreaded the journey to Boggart's Clough. • "Oo's gone to Croft Farm to pray wi' a deein' man," said Mrs. Nutter. _

"How long has he been gone ? " "'Appen ten minutes." "Thank youl " and she turned away. Meanwhile, Alizon rushed along a lonely road towards Boggart's Clough. At first she thought of going to a cabstand, but that she realised would court publicity. The cabman would almost be sure to recognise her; besides, she would gain but little time that way. Under ordinary circumstances she would not have dared to have wandered along this lonely lane in the dark, but she thought nothing of the darkness now. It is true her heart beat wildly, and she dreaded meeting the tramps which were constantly prowling around, but she hurried on through the darkness, and prayed that she might be able to undo the evil she had unwittingly done.

Presently she came to Boggart's Clouii. She had seen nothing of Duncan, she had heard no sound. Her way now would be along, an indistinct footpath towards Croft Farm. WIZ should she say when she overtook him? How could she excuse the action of those of her faith? There was little time to think of these things now, however; her great work was to find him, to warn him. A little later she saw in the gloom of the nipht what she took to be the outbuildings of the farm, and a moment after she heard the sound of voices. Her heart beat even more quickly; in a few minutes she shovld know whether her mission had been successful. Yes, that was Rutland's voice, high-pitched and almost angry. "Kindly let me pass," he said, "a dying man awaits me yonder, and I must see him." "Ther's noa dyin' man yon, mester," was the reply; "that was nobbut a trick. 'Appen yo' did'n' think yo' was sich a fooil." "What do you mean ? " "Aw main as aa thaa'lt noan spaik in th' Indus- trial 'all to-neet."

"Oh, I see, this is a plot to keep me away from the meeting." "Ca' it what yo' plaise, mester. The devil's noan goin' to 'ave it all his on "But I shall go to the meeting," er:ed Duncan, "and it's at your peril you try to stop me." The men laughed in a low, cruel way. "And this is the way you Catholics *defend your faith, I suppose," said Rutland, almost losing control over himself. "Ony trick is geoid eno' to chait the devil," was the reply. "Naa then, lads, is all ready? " Alizon Neville had been creeping nearer to them all the time, and just as she came up she saw two men lay hands on Rutland. "Thou'll noan be sa keen baat tellin' lees abaat th' Cathlicks ageean," she heard one say, savagely, with an oath. "Here, mester, 'ere's summat for yo' to sup." "No, no!" cried Rutland, "take your hands from me." "Tha9. inun 'ave it," was the reply. "To-morra all't Lynford 'll know that Tudor parson wur druffen (drunk) to-fleet." "Stop! Stop!" cried Alizon, scarcely realising what she was saying. "What's you?" was the cry. "By gum, it's a lass! Be quick or we shall be found aat." "Stop! cried Alizon again. "How dare you do such a thing? Shan Kelly, is that you? Aren't you ashamed of yourself? A whisper passed among the men. "It's Miss Alizon," they said ; "but aw thot oo was all right." "Let Mr. Rutland go," continued Alizon. "If you don't your names shall be known, and every- one of you shall be punished. I shall tell Father Sheen, too." There was a laugh, half of derision and half of anger. "Is this the way you defend your faith?" cried the angry girl. "Is this your idea of being true Catholics ? " "We're ony doin' what you wanted," said one of the men, sullenly. "If I have given a wrong impression I am sorry," cried the girl. "I never dreamt that you would interpret my words in such a way. Leave Mr. Rutland alone, or it shall be worse for you." The men stood undecided. .

"Do you think," went on Alizon, "that if you do what you intended, that your purposes will be accomplished? I tell you I will go right back to the meeting and relate exactly what has taken place." She had no fear of these men. Perhaps her position gave her a sense of safety ; perhaps she felt certain that these rough men loved her. Any- how, she had no far, and she stood in their in.(lst without manifesting a shadow of timidity. As for Rutland, he scarcely realised what the episode meant, but he felt perfectly assured of his freedom from harm. 'I he girl's presence was an im- pregnable barrier between him and danger, and while at first he felt angry with his would-be persecutors, be now became calm and considerate. "I should like to know what this means? " he said. "What harm have I done to you ? " _

"It is my fault," cried Alizon, before the man had a chance to reply; and then she hesitated, for she scarce knew what to say. "Your fault!" cried Duncan ; "your fault. Miss Neville! Why, you have stopped those fellows from venting their anger on me. "My fault," repeated the girl, eagerly, "so let me bear all blame." "I do not understand," answered Duncan, bewildered.

"I will tell you," she replied. "I made use of words whieh these men have misunderstood, and— and--"

She hesitated a second, but Duncan understood her. He remembered the confused hints of the colliers, and rapidly piecing the various items of the episode, he saw what it meant. Perhaps the fact that Alizon Neville was a high-born, beautiful girl influenced him to do what he would not have atherwise done, or it might have been that a true, chivalrous nature expressed itself. Anyhow, before Mixon could conclude what she was trying to say, kis mind was made um

••1 beg you will say no more, Miss Neville," he %tried. "I understand everything. I thank you. tory much for troubling to come so far to save me !rom the results of an entire misunderstanding. As for these fellows, they may accept my assurance that nothing more will be heard about the matter." The girl turned towards him gratefully, and in spite of the anger she felt towards him, she could not help admiring him. The men, however, could not understand so clearly. "Dost a main that thou'lt say nowt to th' bobbies ? " said one. _

"I mean that no word concerning the matter shall pass my lips to anyone," said Duncan. "It was a mistake, and this shall be the end of it, as far as I am concerned:" Possibly his words, freeing them from all fear of punishment, quelled their anger, and led them to give up their idea of doing him physical injury, even more than Alizon's presence. Poor, simple fellows, they were not bad-hearted, and they believed they would be serving their faith by making it impossible for Duncan to speak at the meeting. Now, however, they almost forgot their plans, and a more gentle feeling came into their hearts. Thoroughly ashamed of themselves, they slunk away. Most likely they were really glad that their plans had been frustrated, for they were nGt really vicious. Indeed, when they knew that Alizon dis- approved of what they were doing, all their eager- ness had departed, while Duncan's words made it impossible for them to harm him. "Yon's a gradely lad, when all's said and done," remarked one presently. "Yi," said another, " oo's noan so bad. Art a bean to go to th' a'll to-neet ? " "Noa, aw'm noan bean." "NON aw'm noan bean." And then they walked along through the darkness of the night without speaking another word. Meanwhile, Alizon and Rutland stood watching them as they went away. "Thank you, Miss Neville," said Rutland. "Will you allow me to accompany you to the Priory? I shall then ipe in plenty of time for the meeting." "You are very kind," she replied, and then, with many wild -thoughts surging in her mind, she walked away by his side.

CHAPTER VIII.

DUNCAN RUTLAND'S VICTORY.

"Mr. Rutland," said Alizon, when they had walked some distance together, "I must tell you exactly what has taken place." "Pray don't worry," was Rutland's reply; "besides, it is unnecessary. I know. It will only distress you to dwell on it." "You know! How ? " . .

"It requires bu-rlittle penetration to see what it all means," replied the young man. "Let us drop the whole matter; it is not worth dwelling on." "But it is,,, cried the girl ; "and it is due to those men that I tell you. "It was all a mistake, lam sure. For my own part, I only think of one aspect of this night's adventure."

"And that ? " queried Alizon, eagerly. Your courage, your heroism," cried Duncan, fervently. "I know what it meant—that is, I know what it must have cost you to come through the dark night, along a lonely road, to render service to one whom you regard as an enemy to your faith." "Yes, yes," was her answer, "but I could not help it. How c,ould I? I—l, oh, I must tell you —I must! You were so cruel to Father Sheen last week, so bitter towards our faith, Mr. Rutland, that I said, almost without thinking, that I wished something might happen to keep you away from the meeting to-night. One of those men heard me, and so--" "You came all through the darkness to tell them of their mistake. It was wondrously generous of you, Miss Neville, and I thank you. "No, no; of course, I could not help it, but—" She ceased speaking suddenly as though she was frightened at what she was about to say. "Can't you see," she went on presently, and her voice was husky with excitement, "can't you see that you are fighting against God—by—by saying what you did last week? You were encouraging heresy, you were giving arguments to those who will use them as a means of keeping away from the Church. More, you were sowing the seeds of doubt and unrest in the minds of -believers; you were doing untold harm! Can't you see it, Mr. Rutland?"

The young man's heart hardened at her words. "No, I cannot see it.," he replied. "I can substantiate every word I uttered last week. I shall be able to do the same to-night. But let us not talk of that; it would be ungenerous of me." "Oh, please don't think that what you catll arguments have the slightest weight with me," she cried, with a sneer in her voice. "Believe me, my faith is too surely grounded for that." "I was not thinking of that, Miss Neville. I was only thinking how ungenerous it would be of me, after your great kindness, to utter what would be to you rank heresy, and thus hurt yourfeelings." "But you will do so to-night." "That is a different matter. I shall be set for tab defence of the faith that is in me; the people will have come to hear both sides of the matter, and a man who is supposed to be skilled in such matters will be present to answer me. At present I can only think of you as a friend, -a benefactor, who, at great personal sacrifice, has saved me from —I know not what. That is why I said let us speak of something else." "But I cannot—l cannot. Believe me, I can think of nothing else. I—l know Father Sheen is not a good debater, and—and—" "Truth always prevails, Miss Neville. God lives, and Ile never lets the truth suffer. You know what St. Paul said We can do nothing against the truth, but for the truth.' Every false system will die a natural death. Nothing lives, except because of the truth that is in it, for God is Truth." "Do you really believe that?" cried the girl, impulsively. "I am sure of it," replied the young man. "But God uses us to help the truth," she said presently, "and woe be to us if we go against it." "Truth is merciless," replied Duncan. "Then, for your own sake, for the sake of those who will be there, give up this debate," cried the girl, impulsively. "Shall you be there?" asked Duncan. "Yes, I shall be there. I hate the thought of going, but I must go. You have no idea what pain you gave last week." "To you, Miss Neville?" "Yes, to me, and to my mother, and to Father Sheen," cried the girl; impulsively, little realising what she was saying. "I am very sorry," replied Duncan. "It grieves me to give you pain, but—but I cannot help it." "Will nothing persuade you?" cried the girl. "Nothing." She said not another word till they reached the Priory, and then, without a word, save a chilling "Good-night," she left. him, alone. The young man walked towards Lynford, think- ing deeply. "I am afraid this night's experience has unfitted me for a mental contlict,:' he thought; "and it is hard to say what will give pain, but--" "Is that yo', Mester Rutland ? " "It is, Matthy," replied the young man to the query of the old weaver. "Ee—a.w'm reight glad. Aw'm jist bin to yar lodgin's, and when Mrs. Nutter towd me wheer yo'd gone, an' was feared, aw was, fur sure." "Ah, NI-, P. it's all right. You've brought down the books?" "Yi, they're on th' platform by naa. Doan't yo' be inaily-maathed, mester ; yo' gi' et to 'em 'ot. Lots on us er bin prayin' for yo'." "Thank you, Matthy. A few minutes later Duncan found himzelf in the ante-room of the Industrial Hall, where he tried to read his notes, but try as hard as he might, he could not keep the words of Alizon Neville from ringing in his ears, while her face kept constantly corning between his eyes and the words he had written. _ _ . . .

"I shall be a dead failure to-night," he thought, sadly. A few minutes later Father Sheen came into the room, accompanied by some of his supporters. Tile old man looked pale and ill. His eyes were rheutify and bloodshot. His cheeks were flabby, his lips blue. Evidently he much feared the 'encounter, and ought rather to be in bed than at such a meeting. Just before the time for the commence- ment of the debate, Lady Neville and Alizon entered the room. Duncan knew that both had seen him, but neither paid him the slightest attention. Instead, they both talked pleasantly with the old priest. The young man felt depressed, and altogether uneasy. He was ill-prepared for his work. Although Alizon had done her best to nullify her thoughtless words to Shan Kelly, they had more effect than she wot of. They had led to events which had a very disturbing effect on the sensitive, highly-strung young man. Still, he never thought of giving up. He would light the business to the bitter end, and presently, when the chair- man led the way on to the platform, he followed him with a steady step. According to the rules of the debate Duncan had

to spealt nrst on tne second night, and as he sav? the sea of upturned faces he felt that he had uothing to say. After all, why should he give pain? Why should he speak against a faith which, had comforted millions? Why should he earn the enmity of the girl with whom he had been thrown into contact so strangely? When the chairman for the evening arose W reiterate the rules of the debate, and in so doing mentioned his name, he heard, in addition to the cheering, an angry muttering, and he wondered if many there were disappointed at his appearance. This somewhat aroused the fight;ng element within him; but when he rose to speak he felt that, in spite of the enthusiastic applause of his friends, he was ill-preparei for his work. Still, he stammered his way through his speech, and when the half-hour was at an end, sat down without having said anything of much importance. There was no life in his words, no conviction itt his tones ; the address was a failure. Evidently' Father Sheen felt this, for he rose with alacrity and confidence. its fearfulness had departed, his faith rose triumphant. Through the week the old priest had been reading largely of Cardinal Wise- man's addresses, he had also studied Newman care- fully, and he had something to say. As the obi man proceeded, moreover, he became more at ease, his Celtic fervour manifested itself, his words came fluently, and his benignant presence helped his

cause. His peroration, too, was in its way fine. list described the Catholic Church united in its great Head, the Vicegerent of Jesus Christ, and showed how this fact had kept the Church united through the centuries. How that, in spite of stress and storm, enemies without and enemies within, the Church had marched forward. He told of the. noble souls the Church had nurtured. In burning words he described the deeds of heroic souls, deeds. of men who were beacon lights before Proteetantism beg= to be. "And is all this," he cried, "to be put aside for the puny arguments of a fledgling boy, who has. only :lust begun to head the pathway of life, and who happens to be the minister of a so-called Church which forms a part of asect, which, at some time or another, broke away from some other sect, because of some particular fad of some particular man?" He played upon this with rollicking Irish humour, and then, with a passionate appeal, which contained a sneer for Duncan, he sat down amidst loud applause. So far Father Sheen had had the best of the debate. He knew it, Duncan knew it, and the audience knew it. The young minister felt his blood surge madly through his veins as he thought of it, and turning, his eyes met those of Alizore Neville. He saw the look of joyful triumph that shone there. To him the girl's look was a taunt,' a sneer, a scornful challenge, while the tiimultuousl shouts all over the hall told him of his defeat. Perhaps all this was the best thing that could have happened for, Rutland. It put him on his mettle, it made him eager to answer. He forgot Alizon Neville's appeal, and remembered only her look of scorn ; the experiences of the night had lost their power. Facts which he had forgottefl. Caine back to him, arguments: which had evaded him fastened themselves on his memory, and evenY vestige of hesitation had departed. He was able to master himself, too, so that the very derisive, laughter of his opponents sharrened his wits, en 4. made his language more cutting and virile. If his first speech was a failure the second fully' atoned for it, and the fact was fully appreciated lef the audience. His youthful appearance helped' him, too. There was something more than usually striking in the thought that this beardless young. man was doing battle with an old warrior. cheeks were flushed with eagerness, his eyes flashed fire, and every gesture of his lithe young form added to the effect of his words. From the bodf of the hall he looked only a lad of nineteen, and yet he uttered words which swayed the throng of hard-headed men and women as the branches of .1 trees are swayed by a strong wind. He tore Father Sheen's arguments to ,pieces and then threw the fragments from him. He snewed hoer that instead of unity there was discord, and gao hard, irrefutable facts to prove how one fallible Pope had denied the pronouncements of another infallible Pope. He described how 01:1_!. Pope gave a version of the Bible to the world 5; absolutely free from error, and who uttered rt, anathema upon any who should seek to alter, and yet another Pope immediately succeeding, had discovered innumerable errors in it, and then gave the Church another so-called infallible version. He quoted instance after instance of Popes who were the vilest and most cruel of men, who connived at the basest of lies, and sanctioned the most bloody deeds. With fine humour, biting, sarcastic epigrams and burning eloquence, he con- cluded an address which undermined the 01a priest's words, and left him, from an arguments' tive standpoint, bruised, bleeding and helpless. 2_ "By Jove!" said the young lawyer, who ha"' been present the week before, and who had listened to the speech from the standpoint of a barriste! seeking to convince a jury, "it's unanswerable. 1. thought he was going to turn out a frost to-nighty but it's the best thing he's done so far." "It's a pity the chap isn't Lancashire," replied the manufacturer to whom he spoke. "If he were, I'd try and get him to resign Tudor and stand a° our next member. The fellow would make hi° mark in the House of Commons." "I'd rather see him a barrister," was the young solicitor's rejoinder. Father Sheen did not even occupy his half-hour. The old man's mind was confused, and as he heard the murmurs of derision at some of his statement,' he forgot what he was saying and stammered piteously. When he sat down the audience felt that although the debate was only half through, the real issues were settled. Father Sheen had utterlf stumbled at Duncan's statements proving th 4 Papal Infallibility was an insult to the intelligence, a violation of the known facts of history, and ati impious fraud. When Duncan rose to give his final address 11,6 was met with such tumultuous applause as would turn many a young man's brains. At the saute time he heard angry mutterings_, and saw dart vengeful faces, and he knew that but for the presence of a large body of police he would be la great danger. Even as it was he knew that he would, in future' have to watch his every actiott closely, for he had made many enemies. But whatever his failings, cowardice was not OW) of them, and so he faced the people a third time with a clear light in his eyes, and spoke to thens with an unfaltering voice. "To bring this matter to a head," he said, "I purpose to conclude the debate for to-night by con' fining the question to one main issue, and if Fathee Sheen will deal with this issue honestly and fairlf we shall go far to settle the question. Let us tak. the passage in the New Testament on which thie great superstructure of the dogma of Papal Infalli' bility is built." He then quoted the words of Jesus Christ con' cerning Peter, and the Rock on which He should build the Church. On this passage the doctrine of the Papacy we° built, and the Roman Church had claimed tho Peter's powers had been transmitted to the succeed° ing Popes ever since. He then went on to shelf, that for three hundred years not one writer hal ever ventured to quote these words as applying °I the Bishop at Rome, not one Pope had ever use,' it as applying to,himself ; rather the great writ6r! of the first three- centuries had eliewn that it DO an entirely different application. Then, in (ode to establish its claim the Roman Church had, 111 later years, issued manuscripts• called o,_l "Clementine Homilies" and the "Isidort Decretals." These pretented to shew that there he' been a continuous succession of Bishops back to the' time of Peter. The later Popes' had accepted thee' Homilies and Decretals, and had placed thir: imprimaturs upon them. Now every scholee admitted that both the Homilies and Decretelcet were forgeries. And thus Infallible Popes he again and again declared true, what had bee° again and again proved to be forgeries and lies. An abler man might have bolstered up somethia like a case. He might, like a popular priest 14.1 is related to a great dignitary, have pleaded that 1, Was not likely that millions of people thro% hundreds of years would have been mistaken. Pt might have poured forth turgid rhetoric, b,llO Father Sheen was utterly crushed. He was reader of Church history. He was unable to refin; Duncan's arguments; and when he sat down, af.t,e_. uttering some feeble expletives, he knew that Catholic faith had been but miserably defendii% He knew that much might have been said, 0e1914 was unable to say it, and he felt with sorrow tufd even the adherents of his church would go 51"/ confused and unsettled in their minds. "Mother, I hate him," Alizon said to ritodle Neville as they drove up to the Priory door. may be clever, lam afraid he is, but I hate him-1 He will do untold harm to our church." o. "T• m he issue is in God's hands, y darlint, replied her ladyship; "let us pray for Rio

The Condition of Warrington Roads.

Strong Complaints.—Better Roads Promised

It was decided at a meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee that Palmyra-square, between the Wesleyan Chapel and Queen's Gardens, be laid with tarred macadam. The following report of the Borough Surveyor was read:—ln accordance with your resolution dated May 2810, 1900, " That the Borough Surveyor rvort upon the cost of laying down wood pavement in Napier-street,and tee comparative cost of tar pavement" I now beg to report that I have considered the question in all its bearings, particularly as to noiselessness. The present carriage-way paving of Napier-street is composed of 385 scpaare yards of macadam, and 800 square yards or sett paving, making a total of 1,685 square yards. The sett paving is composed of 4-inch granite cubes, and has been down for a period of about 30 years. The present paving material in any cis • of renewal will have to be removed. There are only two classes of pavement suited for this street if the primary object is to be noise- lessneas,viz :-Is.—Tarred macadam pavernent.l would Pr pose to put in a 4-inch bed of concrete, and a 3-inch bed of macadam, covered with one inch of /inch tarred macadam. This would cost 53 61 per yard, or a total of £463 74 6d. 2nd—Wood block paving. Six-inch wood block paving, with foundation, will cost 12s 91 per yard or a total of £1,069 3,9 d. The maintenance of the tarred macadam pavement would cost about 10 per cent and that of wood 5 per cent. per annum, lam of opinion that wood Week paving would Le the less noisy. It was resolved that Napier-street be paved with wood, providing the Sanitary Works Committee are prepared to pay the cost. Coot cillor TINNION, in moving the confirmation of the minutes, referred to the proposal to pave Palmyra- square between the Wesleyan Chapel and Quaen's Gardens with tarred macadam. A request was made for wood pavement but the committee thought the pro- posal would meet all requirements. With regard to Napier-street, the committee had nothing in their esti- mates to cover that kind of work, and further it was felt that that was hardly the kind of work they should be compelled to undertake under ordinary circumstancee. Neither did they wish to establish an awkward prece- dent, and the speaker gave one or two instances to show that they would be placed in an awkward posi- tion if the request of the Sanitary Works Committee were agreed to. Councillor Tinnion proceeded to state that complaints had been received from cyclists as to the condition of some of the roads in the borough. Cyclists were very nice people in their way, but if the roads were made as they would wish them to be made they would have to put something down in the shape of glass with a minimum of friction. (Laughter.) He hoped after the committee had dealt with the macadam roads in the way it was intended to deal with them that there would be less complaint. It was felt by the com- mittee that there were some complaints which might .be just, but they would do their utmost during the next few weeks te remedy the sxisting state of things. (Hear. hear.) Turkish Baths.

It was resolved at a meet:.ng of the Bathe Committee that the tender of Mr. Thomas Pilkington, of Rainford, Ear the erection of Turkido baths and the alterations to the exlsting baths, at £6,627 4s. 11d., be accepted, sub- ject to the approval of the Local Government Board, and to the contractor furnishing satisfactory references. Alderman Boum, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said they bad several tenders for the erection of Turkish baths and alterations to the existing baths, and the one accepted was the lowest, and very con- siderably so. The amount was 20 psr cent more than they originally estimated °wine to the increased price of materials acd labour.but the committee considered it to be very reasonable as architects always estimated at the lowest possible figure. Plans were submitted for approval. Couecillor ELLISON seconded. Councillor BRAM GREEN suggested the postponement of the proposal. If the increase of prices representee 20 per cent. no harm would be done if the scheme were postponed for a year or two. An expenditure represent- ing a penny in the pound for Turkish baths was a eerious addition. Unless they could have something like a reasonable return for this expenditure he did not think it should be undertaken at the present moment. He had no idea what number of people would use the batbsoand thought some inquiries mighs b 3 made into the matter of the probable return before so large on expenditure was incurret lie did not wish to move an amendment, but would ask the Chairman of the Committee if he did not consider it desirable to withdraw the proposal for the present. Councillor F R ROBERTS wished that Councillor Shaw Green had moved an amendment. There was a great dealof criticism in the town regarding the proposal,and the baths were considered almost altogether useless. The people requiring Turkish baths were very few indeed. He had never had one, and he did not know that he would ever want to. (Laughter), He understood that the up-keep would be very serious. Before spending this enormous sum, representing a penny in the pound they ought to hesitate, especially in these times of enor- mously increased taxation. This was a luxury very few of the inhabitants required, and if Councillor Shaw Green would not move an amendment he would move that the minutes be confirmed with the exception of the one relating te the erection of Turkish baths and altera- tions to the existing baths. Councillor A J JOLLEY seconded. He reminded the Council that several other matters were coming to the front, in connection both with the Corp oration aed the Union, which would involve heavy expenditurematters which were of more consequence than the provision of Turkish baths, though be did not object to them per se. Be hoped the Council would refer this minute back. Councillor BENNETT thought the proposal of the Corn- mitten a good one. The principle bad been ancroved by the Council,and now it was only a quesion of figures. Toe provision of these baths was very desirable. The peeple generally could be educated up to enjoy a Turk ish bath ard realise its importance from the point of- view of health. They would induce personal cleanliness, and the ordinary bath was not enough for many ot the population. ("Oh, oh.") This proposal deserved encnur- agement and support. It was only a matter of £3.000, and if it would enable the people to have this luxury with its advantages at a comparatively reasonable out- lay he thought it deserved sepport. Councillor TINNION did not onject to the principle but People who wanted a Turkish bath could go over to Manchester or Liverpool much better than the funds of the borough would at the present time suoport the erection and maintenance of the baths. They were spending so much ilmoney on other matters that were absolutely indispensable that it would be desirable that this proposal should be postponed for some time. Ho would like to know whether the higher prices of labour and material solely had entailed this 20 per cent. more cost Alderman Bovr-N said the estimate was £5,400 and , the tender was £6,621 4s 1.1. d. That was not quite 20 per cent., and was accounted for partly by the increased price of the materials and labour and partly by the fact that the architects had given them the lowest estimate. Councillor Moexs did not consider it wise at the present time to incur the expense. The increase in some materials during the last three years bad been 75 per cent. Alderman FRANCOMB believed that the baths would be self-paying. One of the finest baths in the country was st a little town in the potseries—Burslem. At Stock- port they had splendid baths. much cheaper than Man- chester or Liverpool. This question had been on at least ten years. He thought the quotation a reasonable one. The Turkish baths would only cost £6,000, and that represented. only a halfpenny in the pound. Be considered that they shotnd keep pace with the times. Alderman Bovron reminded the Council of the alterations that were necessary to the present buildings. The slipper baths were is a bad condition, while the second class bath would disgrace any baths in the king- dom. The question was whether they were to have Turkish baths in addition to carrying out this other wore. The elder people of the town who paid rates were entitled to be catered for as well as the younger generation. The committee did not want the extra syork that these baths would bring, and the Council had approved of the principle, and they were ready to carry It out in a proper manner. There was no guarantee that prices would be cheaper twelve months hence. Council- lor Shaw Green always looked upon the black Fide of financial proposals unless he was sT.endinz the money himself. (Laughter.) lie (Alderman Bolton) thought Turkish baths of more consequence than two acres of land at Market-gate. The MAYOR said he had supported consistently for many years the proposal to erect Turkish baths, and he was bound on every ground to support the minutes of the committee. Tao eternal rate which was continually introduced when any question of expense which was not directly likely to be self-supporting was brought forward, was one of those bogies they could well afford to discount to a very considerable extent. If they did nut receive a direct financial result, sufficient to meet the expense, at any rate they would receive a very great advantage from the use of the baths. On being put to the vote ten voted for the amendment and twelve against it. The minutes were then put and approved.

Rivers Committee. Proposed Meeting of Manufacturers and

WARRINGTON TRAMWAYS BILL. The Bill Passed.

On Tuesday afternoon a Bill to confirm the pro- visional order authorising the Corporation of Warring- ton to construct tramways catle before a Select Com- mittee of the House of Lords, presided over by the Duke of Northumberland. Counsel for the promoters were Mr. RAM, Q C., and Mr. Hutchinson. The peti- tioners were ttse London and N irch-Western Railway Company, represented by Mr. Moon. Mr. RAM said the object of the provisional order which the Corporation had obtained was to enable them to supply tramways within their borough. The town of War, ington was one of very considerable import- ance and size. I:, was incorporated in 1847, exteaded in 1890, and again in 1896 It had recently, by order of the Local Government Board, been made a county borough, atad a Bill confirming that order was now before the House of Lords. According to the last census, the population was 52,742. There was very Large traffic between Warrington and the neighbouring places and a very important mid running south crossed the Mersey by a bridge. Of late a good deal had been done by the way cf widening the streets in order to make feasible a system of tramways in Warriogton and a very great need existed for them. Several attempts had been made to provide tram ways for the town. In 1878, a company endeavoured to provide a system, but failed to obtain the necessary power, principally on the ground that the streets were too narrow. 3,nother attempt was made in 1882, this also failing, but the fact that the attempt was made showed that there was a belief on the part of the promoters that there was much need for them, and that the speculation would be a remunerative oae. The Corporation had now come to the conclusion that the time had arrived when, as the principal local authority, they should move in the matter. There were 65 hackney carriages and 35 omnibuses licensed by the Corporation. which plied between the town and neighbouring places; but although the Corporation had the power to license these vehicles, they had no power to compel them to run, or to cause them to run, at a certain time. There was consequently a crying need for more public convey- ances. Concerning tramway No. 10, which certainty was the most important section, and which had aroused some opposition, the London and North-Western Rail- way Company objected to the crossing of the level, on the ground that it would be a constant source of danger and most prejudicial to the cenvenient working of toe railway traffic. Be quite admitted the necessity of ade- quately meeting the objec:ion,, and if it was coosidered necessary to erect a bridge, the Corporation would be prepared to pay their share of the cost. The promoters asked that the No. 10 train should be a double line; but, to meet the wishes of the railway company, they were willing that it should be a single line only across the level. Generally speaking, there was a remarkable ab- stinence of hostility to the Bill, the only opposition, practically,being that of the London and North-Western Railway Compapy. He submitted that the promoters had shown every desire to meet the company on reason- ab!eo terms, and he appealed to the Committee to pass the_preamble. _

Major Cardew, the engineer, in giving evidence, said the Board of Trade required as a condition of the con- s:ruction of the tramway on the level crossing that the consent in writing of the Board of Trade should be obtained. In nis opinion, it would be perfectly practic- able to cirry out the seliem as proposed by the pro- m:iters. They did not expect to ba able to run a quick s,-ervice of trams, arid thy were prepared to underke that cars should proceed o7er the level crossing at a walking pies. Witnesses were then called for the promoters„.

Mr. Thomas Longdin, borough surveyor of Warring- ton, also gave evidence, and said tramway No. 10 could be carried over the level crossing in a manner consistent with public safety. Mr. Moon, for tbe London and Nortb-Western Railway Company, said he did not think it eould be contended that the crossing of a railway by an electric tramway was a crossing that could be carried on withlut danger to the public. It became a question whether the danger was so great as to induce the Committee to say that havini, regard to the advantages which the tratnways woulirget they would not pass the Bill. If the Bill were passed, the traffic in faturss would increase and the dan- ger at the crossing would become intens'ficid. He asked their lordships to say whether they thought it desirable that this tra,nway should pass at all across the crossing. In the result, the Chairmn announced that the Com- mittee had decided to pass the bill, i.ncluding tramway No. 10 as it'stood, but with the addition of the proviso that if the Board of Trade should order a bridge :to he made the Co:poration of Warrington should contribute one-half of the cost.

THE COMPENSATION CLAIM FOR THE LOSS OF AN EYE.

Messrs. Crosfield's Appeal.

On Saturday the Court of Appeal, consisting of Lord Justices A. L. Smith, Vaughan Williams, and Romer, heard the appeal of the plaintiffs Crosfield and Sons, Limited, V. Patrick Tanian, from the refusal of the County Court judge of Warrington to review an award trade by his predecessor under the Workmen's Compen- sation Act of 1897. Mr. Arthur Powell appeared for the appellants, and Mr. Benson for the respondent. Mr. Powell said that Patriek Tanian was a labsuier in the employ of Messrs. Croefield and Sons, when he nett with an accident and le,se his right eye. In his request for arbitration Tenian alleeed that his average weekly earnings were 30s and that after the accident he earned 17s a • eek. Both before and after the request for arbitration had been filed negotiations were going on between the man and the masters, but no arrangement , was come to owing to these negotiations. The employers ' neglected to put in the;r answer to the claim five clear days before the arbitration, and when the matter came betore the Clump Court judge the poi .t was taken that the employers' answer, which alleged that the man's average weekly earnings were only 191., could not be taken into consideration. The learned judge upheld that contention, and took the average weekly earnings as sta.ed by the man himself, ordering a weekly payment ot 12s 61. After the payrn -nt had been continued for some time the employers applied to have it reduced on the ground that the award bad been made under the mistaken impression that the workman's average weekly earnines were 30i. The learned judge, however, dis- missed the appliceti sn, and the employers now ap ,ealed. Without calling upon counsel for the respondent Lord Justice Smith gave judgment. He said the question raieed depended upon clause 12 of the first edit:sr:We to the Act. Putting it shortly, the question was whether the employers could have a review ot the weekly pay- ment ordered when the circumstances which exist-d at the date of the award had not altered in the slightest degree. The real point taken by the employers wes that the award Was wrong because the learned judge ought to have found rile man's average weekly earnines to he 17s. instead ot 304. and they relied upon clause 12 of the first schedule to the fact. In hie opinion, that clause provided that where the circumstances altered there might be a review of the award. In this case there were no altered circumstances, and the eniplosers were not entitled to A review ct the award. The appeal would therefore be dismissel with costs.

Lord Justice Romer, in concurring, said that if they wore to accale to the appellants' contention it would be as good as holding that whenever a party was dis- satisfied with an award he could from time to time apply to the County Court to have it reviewed until he got something in his favour. Lord Justice Wigiams disagreed. Upon the true con- struction of the clause in question he was of opinion that the appellants were entitled to. have the award reviewed. • The appeal was dismissed with costs.

WARRINGTON WALKING DAY.

The Processions.

Walking Day! The mere mention of the name of this popular Warrington festival sends a thrill of delight through the younger portion of the com- munity, and to many of tee older inhabitants it is associated with pleasant reminiscences. It may be said to add a dash of colour to the dull-grey monotony of life in a manufacturing town such as Warrington, and if for no other reason than that, it is to be heartily welcomed. It is impossible to describe with what pleasurable anticipations the near approach of this day of days is viewed by the children. " What are you going to wear on Walking Day ? " is a familiar expression long before the appointed day, and this subject of dress is one which calls forth the thoughtfulness and willing assistance of the parents. The beautiful dresses which were to be seen in such great pro- fusion on Friday could not be possible without the expenditure of a large amount of hard cash and in many cases it involves much self-sacrifice on the part ot the parents. They are rewarded to some extent by witnessing the living wealth of colour and beauty as it traverses the streets on Walking , Day and in the expressions of happiness so mani- festly apparent on the faces of the children. Warrington always looks at its best on Walking I Day. On Friday it wore its annual festive appear- ance and was as gay and bright as on any previous festival. Flags and banners floated in various parts of the town, and the whole length of the line of procession was bedecked with bunting. Here and I there lines of flags stretching across the streets flattered gaily in the gentle breeze. During the early part of the week the weather was very un- settled but there was a decided improvement on Thursday. On Friday,the morning opened brightly but towards eleven o'clock there were signs of rain. Fortunately, however,the weather continued fine until alter two o'clock, and the processions were not interfered with. A steady drizzle con- tinued throughout the afternoon and it was not until six o'clock that the weather took a favourable change. The remainder of the eveeing continued fine.

By nine o'clock the public began to assemble along the line of the route of the procession, and when the scholars walked along the streets in what seemed an endless stream of charming little fairies rejoicing in their beauty and attire, the whole pre- sented an animated spectacle. One of the prettiest sights was that witnessed from the Town Hall steps. From this coign of vantag,/ a good view was obtained of the schools as they entered and left the Park. The assembly on the lawn was one of the sights of the day, and was as picturesque a scene as one could well wish for.

There was no combined procession of all the schools in tile town, such as was witnessed in cele- bration of the Queen's Diamond Jubilee. Most of the Nonconformlst schools left by early trains for pleasant spots in the immediate neighbourhood.For instance,BoP-3treet VVesleyans journeyed to Lymm whilst their friends at Silver-street school spent the day at lielsbs. Wycliffe scholars proceeded to Preston Brook ; Cairo-street to Grappenball ; St. John's Presbyterian TO Frodsham and Working- men's Mission to Walton Lea. Tne Catholic schools, in accordance with their usual custom in Warring- ton had h separate procession of their Own, so that the assembly in front of the rown Hall was largely confined to the Church of England schools. . . _

The scholars commenced to arrive on the lawn shortly before ten o'clock and the onerous duties of marshals were satisfactorily discharged by Mr R T Fairclough and Cap:•ain B Fairclough. Mr J Craik, who has assisted in the work for many years was absent on this occaion through illness. Perfect order was kept by the Chief Constable and his staff which numbered 57 men and they are to be con- gratulated upon the excellent manner in which the arrangements were carried out. The erection of the Walker memorial fountain has somewhat re- stricted the accommodation on the lawn but no in- convenience was experienced on this account. In fact, the fountain with its cooling sprays playing in the sunlight gave an added attractiveness to the spectacle. Several schools were late in arriving but a fevt, minutes after ten o'clock it was wisely decided not to keep the children waiting and the signal was given for the first school to move off. The Blue Coat School occupied the place of honour and headed by their own band they filed through the central arch. In the forefront was the banner of the school,indicating that the charity was founded. so far back as 1677. Another banner bore the inscription " Fear God ; Honour the King The usual route was taken and the arrangements for lurries to rejoin the procession were the same as in previous years. These substantial vehicles with their human freight of smiling youngsters were taken down the Western drive and rejoined their respective schools as the procession proceeded on its way to Arpley-streat. All the lurriei had been more or less decorated and it was evident that con- siderable pains had been taken to make them as artistic as possible. The following was the order in which the schools left the Park :

1 Blue Coat School and Band 2 Padgate Industrial Schools and Band 3 Parish Church and Lymm Church Band 4. St. George's Mi4sion and Walton Band 5 St. Clement's Mission 6 St. Paul's and Band 7 All Saints Mission 8 Holy Trinity and "Guardian " Baud 9 Fierce's- Yard Mission 10 St. Luke's Schonl 11 St. James' and Volunteer Band 12 S. Ann's and Band 13 St. Barnabas and Fletcher's Band 14 St. Peter's and Crosfield's Band 15 Emmanuel 114ormed Church 16 Legh street Primitive Methodist 17 Latchford Primitive Methodist 18 Sharpe street Primitive Methodist 19 Bucklev'street Indep7ndent.; Methodist and Band 20 Salvation Army and Band 21 Ragged School and Band 22 Sanke7 Bridges Wesleyan and Penketh Tannery Band.

There were few special features in connection I with the display of the schools. Very few innova- tions were introduced and as a result the proces- sion was much the same as in previous years. Sonic very pretty dresses were worn and by way of con- trast it was interesting to take notice of the boys and girls of ragger•tatterdom who composed the procession for the most part of the Salvation Army 1 and Ragged School. The children from Par!gate I Industrial Schools—the boys with their black cor- duroy suits and white collars and the girls in dresses of navy blue serge with lace collarettes— looked neat and comfortable. There was a I pretty group of boys and girls near the head of the'l Parish Church procession. The girls were dressed in old English style with cream smocks and Dutch I bonnets, and two of them held the train of a maiden , queen wearing a crown of roses, who walked with becoming grace- and dignity. Very appropriately, some sailor lads were in attendance and they looked very smart in their suits of white and blue. Some choice banners were carried,and noticeable amongst them were several fine ones in connection with I Holy Trinity school. At the head of this school's attractive procession was a representative of a trooper in khaki. In St. James' school procession a number of coloured pictures illustrat• ing important events in religious history attracted universal attention. Here and there in the pro- c:ssion some floral crowns were displayed on the top of poles and suspended from them were ribbons held by daintily attired children. Others carried bunches of marguerites whilst a number had baskets of beautif flowers. Decorated mail carts were once again to the fore in several schools and prettily dressed children were to be seen cosily ensconced under canopies of bright material. In connection with St. Peter's Church the Church Lad's Brigade who marched in soldierly style headed the proces- sion. Prominent in the Buckley-street procession was a lurry on which were characters representing the good feeling existing between John Bull and his brother Jonathan surrounded by the represen- tatives of the colonies.

It took exictly an hour for the procession to file out of the park the number of children who passed thrrugh the gates being a small increase on last year. The Mayor and Mayoress were in attendance and a large number of local ladies and gentlemen witnessed the proceedings from the Town Hall steps. After proceeding along the appointt d route the scholars attended service at their respective churches. In the afternoon they adjourned to &ids where they disported themselves until the con- tinued rain put a stop to their further enjoyment. The procession of Roman Catholics has always been a brilliant display, but it may safely be said that this year's eclipsed all previous efforts. As the pageant wended its way through the principal streets of the town it excited the genuine admira- tion of the thousands of onlookers, and some of the

!spectators even gave vent to their appreciation by applauding. The four Catholic schools in the town assembled at Bridge Fcot at about 11.30 —immediately after the conclusion of the Church of England and Nonconformist procession. The scholars instead of walking two by two proceeded in two separate lines on either side of the street— Indian filejand produced a pretty effect. The children were most tastefully attired and the dresses bore testimony to the painstaking care that must have been exercised in their preparation. White dresses with scarlet sashes were worn by sicholars of one school, whilst others were distinguished by violet, purple and blue bands. Each school was preceded by the priests and exceptionally beauti- ful banners were displayed. Some bore pictures , representing the different Sacraments, and the ! ribbons were carried by prettily dressed girls. Several bands—including the Borough Band, St. Joseph's Industrial School, Stockport and the League of the Cross—discoursed lively music and the discipline of the children was a feature of the procession. The smallest child instinctively " marked-time " whenever there was a momentary halt, and the whole affair reflected the greatest credit on those responsible. Some ex- quisite floral devices were carried. A number of charmingly dressed young ladies bore a large cross of roses, whilst a harp surrounded by gaily coloured ribbons was much admired. Most of the little children were bedecked with a garland of flowers on their heads and carried choice bouquets; and a number of boys wore hats bearing such words as "Pretoria" and "Ladysmith." The procession pre- sented an extremely pretty spectacle and took about a quarter of an hour to pass a given point. Ser- vices were afterwards held at the several Catholic churches,

!DROWNING FATALITIES

AT WARRINGTON.

Another Boy Drowned in the St. Helens Canal.

Inquests.

An inquest was conducted by Mr. S. Brighouse,county coroner, at the Police Station on Saturday morning on the body of James Henry Moores, aged seven.of Widnes, which was found in the river at Sankoy on Thursday morning. The body had apparently been in the water about six months. James Moores, a waterman living at 29. John-street, • Widnes, identified the body as that of his son by the clothes, &o. Deceased was 7i years of age when he was missed on December 26M last. He was last seen at 1.30 p.m. on that day on Widnes Pier Head playing with some other boys. The pier was near their house, and after school hours children generally went to play there. When he did not return at night witness gave informa- tion to the Whines police. William Thompson. Sankey, stated that at about tea o'clock on Thursday morning last he saw the body of the deceased on the bank of tilt., river at Sankey. Be in- formed the police, and the body was removed to the Black Horse lon. A verdict of "Found Drowned" was returned.

Boy Drowned at Latchford. A Suggestion.

The same morning Mr. Brighouse held an inquiry into the circumstances attending the death of a boy named David Connor,aged seven years,son of Susannah Connor, a widow, residing at 24, Cockhedge-lane, Warrington. The boy was playing on the bank of the Bridgewater Canal at Latcnford and in attempting to get a stick out of the water he overbalanced himself, fell in, and was drowned.

A boy named David Nolan, cousin of the deceased,said that abnut one o'clock on Thursday afternoon he and the deceased paid a visit to their grandfather, who was working at Latchford. They left him at 2.15 and went down the caual bank. While there the do_eased knelt on the bank and tried to reach a piece of wood which was in the water, but overbalanced himself and fell into the water. He told his grandfather,and with the assist- ance of Mr. Nolan, decease.d was git out. Be was then dead. They were the only boys ou the canal bank. Thomas Nixon, of the Black Bear Inn, deposed to a,sisting in getting the body of the deceased out of the water, It had been in 12 minutes.

Inspector Phillips said the boy would be poisoned by the water. It was gs black as ink. A verdict of " Accidental death" was returned. Mr. Nixon asked if it would not be a wise thing to have a grappling hook, life-buoy, or some other appli- ances for saving life near the bridge. If there had be one on toe presunt occasion the deceased %could probably have been saved.

The Coroner : Have many people been drowned at this r 3 pot ?

Mr. Nixon: Yes, a good many. In reply ta the Coroner, witness said that the appli- ances would have to be provided by the Manchester Ship Canal Comnany. who o%ned that canal. There were some grappling irons about half a mile away, but while one was going for those, a person could be drowned. His house was a few yards away from the canal, and he was willing to take care of any such appliance.

The Coroner suggested that he (the Coroner), should write to cba Manchezta bhip Canal Company, explain- ing the circumstances of the case, and saying that Mr. Nixon was willing to take c are of any appliances for saving life. There was no legal obligation. This was agreed to. Another Boy Drowned in the St. Helens and Sankey Canal.

At 12-59 on Sunday afternoon the body of a by named Joseph Parkinson, son of John Henry Parkinson, an insurance agent of 38. Baxter-street, vas recovered from the St. Helens and Sankey Canal. The deceased had been missing from home since 3.30 on Thursday afternoon. Dragging operations were commencNi in the canal on Sunday morning, and the body was recovered by John Jackson, a boatman. The inquest was con- ducted by Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, at the Police Station on Tuesday.

. - John Henry Paikluson. deceased's father, said his son was six years of age. Ho left the house on Thursday afternoon and went out to play and never returned. He was brought home dead on Sunday at a quarter to one. The previous week deceased was playing by the canal and his muther punished him for doing so. Elizabeth Appleton, who keeps a small ship at 7, Baxter-street, said the deceased called at 6 o'clock on Thursday evening and bought an orange. There was a nother little boy with him,!but ehe did not notice who he was. The distance from her shop to the canal was about a quarter of a mile.

John Jackson, a boatman, residing a: 27, Howard- street. Sankey Bridges, said he drafmed the canal on Sunday and recwered the body at 12 50. It was fully dressed. A great many children played about the canal. Inspector Phillips temarked that the canal was not within the jurisdiction of the police,as it was beyond the borough boundary.

The Coroner said that parents cw:ht to punish their children when they played about the canal. A verdict ef "Foiled drowned " was returned.
