SERIOUS FIKES.

Stacks Destroyed at Sankey.

Supposed lncendiarism.

At ten o'clock on Sunday night a fire broke out on the premises of Mrs. Crosby, Lingley Green Farm, Great Sankey. The Warrington Fire Brigade was at once sum- moned and turned out under the direction of Deputy Superintendent Turner with the steam enginellajor. Oa arrival at the scene of the conflagration they found that two stacks—one hay and one straw—were ablaz.a. For- tunately the wind was not blowing in the direction of the farm, or the whole of the buildings might have been destroyed. After working two or three hours the flames were got under, damage having been done to the extent of £lOO. The stacks were insured in the Westminster Office. The firemen returned to Warrington at 3.30. The confligration illuminated the sky fir miles around and attracted large crowds of people from St. Helens. Widnes, Warrington, and surrounding district. The fire is supposed to have been the work of an incendiary. Stables Gutted in Tanners'-lane.

At twenty minutes to one on Monday morning Mr. Owen Toole, a marine store dealer of 57, Allen-street was passing along Erwood-atreet on is bicycle when he noticed flames issuing from the second fhor of the stables of Mr. E tward Isherwood, contractor, Tanners'- lane, Warrington. He at once rang the fire bell and Mr. Isherw)od who lives in a house adjoining the Prewises got up. Inspector Taykr, S2rgeant Hollingworth and P.C.7Phillips were s'ion on the scene and 16 horses were removed from the stables and some documents were taken frcm the office. PVC. Phillips was in the act of handing a book thri.ugh the office window when he cut his hand severely. He was obliged to go to the Infirmary and have the wound stitched. Superintendent Turner and a complement of firemen turned out with the fire engine" Captain." Tiler, was a good supply of water and the fire was extinguished, but not before the premises had been gutted. Fortunately the dimes were prevented from spreading to the adjoining Tannery and what would have been a much more serious conflagra- tion was averted.

Another Stack Fire at Penketh. A fire broke out in the stack-yard of Mr. Joseph Wright's fa: m—Chapel Brow Farm—Great Sankey, at ten o'clock on Monday morning. The Warrington Fire Brigade was summone 1, and on their arrival at he farm found tw,) large stacks of hay blazing fi(rcely: A third was in danger of being ignited,aud the first efforts were directed towards saving it. In this the brigade were stccessful. One of the other two stacks was partially saved, but the fire completely destroyed the third. A very fortunate circumstance was that there was no wind at the titne, otherwise the whole contents of the stack-y.ird would inevitable have been destroyed, and St. Mary's Cturcb, which is do e by, wolf d have been in greit danger. The fire is supposed to have been caused by an in- cendiary. The damage, which is eAimated at £l5O, is covered by insuranco.

Dividend.

The directors of the Lancashire and Yorkshire Bank have declared an interim dividend for the past half-year of 14s per share, being at the rate of 14 per cent, per annum. Last year the interim divi- dend was 12s. per share.

LIVERPOOL.

THE country will not The South African allow an Government to Hospital Inquiry.

. play with so serious a mat- ter as an investigation into the measures taken to deal with fever cases in military hospitals in South Africa. Nothing in the form of a depart- mental committee will suffice. It will be suspec- ted, and it will not command the assent of the House of Commons. The conduct of the Govern- ment is implicated, as well as arrangements made in the field and at stationary hospitals. The best informed persons are aware that the treatment of enteric fever was made the subject of special re- presentations to the War Office early in the cam- , paign, and that two suggestions were presented— the first in favour of a supervisory civilian hospital committee, the second in favour of a special body of medical experts being selected for the special I treatment of enteric fever cases. Both schemes were rejected, with the result that no independent supervision was made, and that enteric cases were left to one or two doctors and some male nurses, who were, generally speaking patient 9 recovering from illness. Tao Government may make out a case for declining the former, but since it selected competent civil surgeons to look after the wounded it cannot excuse itself for not being equally careful of the sick, who became much more numerous. No departmental committee could enter upon tbeso preliminary inquiries, and yet they are a part of the whole case. The count*/ will give no confidence to any but a thoroughlY impartial committee, because it wants a complete and reliable investigation. A sham inquiry, limited to Bloemfontein, will not do. It was the worst case of neglect, in all probability, but the case of Ladysmith cannot ,be excluded, and even 0 Kimberley it may be necessary to prosecute inquiries. The constitution of the Committee WU be as important as the terms of reference,and upon each point any attempt to secure a favourah.le judgment by manoeuvring beforehand will be resis- ted by the Opposition and condemned hYthe country. The exposure having now been made° and Mr. BURDETT-COUTTS'S complaints and details , having been corroborated and supplemented bY independent testimony, we are in a position to sal that the Government defence is inadequate,and that everything, from top to bottom, must be subject te investigation. The Government ought to see, politi- (ally, that more harm will be done to it by policy of evasion and suppression than by one et frankness and straightforwardness. The excuse hitherto offered can only be accepted if they ara found to be based upon facts. Mr. WyNniaadS defence is not,final, and it ought to be impossiole for anybody to suspect that the object of the Government is to bolster up that defence rather than to lay bare the truth, let the consequehces be what they may. The surprise of the Governtnen that anybody should question the organisation it had set up for hospitals, and that at Bloemfontein under the eye of Lord ROBERTS himsolf, graVe scandals should have occurred, and men should have died for want of adequate medical attention and nursing, may be natural and not affected. 13'1 it is part of the case against it that it seemed to be' unaware that anything had gone amiss, and that lives were being daily lost that ought to have been saved, and would have been saved, by the simple-4 provisions. The relatives of the dead and the in' valided seem to have the strongest claim for 91 perfect inquiry, and they cannot be lett out of the question. Blame will be found to rest somewhere, and the investigators have to -,discovor who were responsible and what ought to have been done • Ignorance cannot be pleaded, and the plea et military exigency is too shallow to cover negler° when no operations were proceeding.

The allied fleet would have no power given to it that it is not already exercising. Moreover, the attitude of watchfulness adopted in other parts of China would come to an end the moment war Was declared. At Shanghai and on the Yangtse generally, at Canton and at Hong Kong, the Chinese troops and population would precipitate themselves on foLvigners generally,and a great con- flagration would begin. The proclamation already issued, in which our efforts were defined as limited to the protection of Ambassadors and foreign residents would be set at naught, and the Chinese would have reason to complain of bad faith. We profess to be dealing with a local trouble, and that is a trio statement of the case. It would be most impolitic to depart from that attitude. The entry of the allies into Pekin may result in a change of government and the gradual restoration of order and peace. To abandon that /lope would be most inexpedient. We have no right to assume that we cannot enter Pekin ; that a declaration of war would assist the Chinese in the capital and its vicinity to offer us reasonable terms and respect international obligations; or that, in the event of there being conflicting parties 111 the capital, such an act would help to the front the party least desirous of war with the Powers arid most anxious for peace. An actual state of War, further, does not and need not exist in other Parts of China. Directions from Pekin have been disobeyed, there is a pause, and we had better Make the best of it by continuing to act upon the Ufa lines laid down. When Pekin has been reached and is in full possession of the allies the immediate question will be the establishment of a G-overnment likely to be respected there and all over China. Declaring war would unite all China against us, magnify the problem of conquest, necessitate the employment of large armies, and lead up to what We are anxious ;Jo avoid —chaos and partition.

Flippant paragraphs intended to b 3 humorous occasionally appear in the English press regarding the occupations chosen by women in Atnerica, No tale has been previously given so complete as that prepared by a lady writer for this month's Humanitarian," and recorded there not as a source of amusement, but for edification and ex- ample. Two hundred and fifty women figure as Ministers, having charges, preaching and doing regular parish work. One, the Rev. Lydia A. Saxton, has been a chaplain for nearly fifty years, Which disposes of the idea that a woman preacher Will be tolerated only so long as she has the at- tractive grace of maidenhood. Then there are Women architects by the score, and at least one fully-certificated woman pilot. The Chicago directory shiws that fifteen in that city are druggists, while in the legal profession throughout the States they have long ceased to be a novelty. The joke about their Powers of special pleading has become ancient, but with it, of course, has gone in these cases the tradition as to extra courtesy. There must be no favour in the courts, and they claim none. The Ingot important consideration, however, for both sexes is that their remuneration is no lower than that of the men. In factory and shop work in England the employment of women has tended to lower the rate of wages, .and so to make it more difficult for the men willing to bear tho double burden to maintain a household. The most satisfying life for a woman is in the home, and these exCursions into more arduous fields are rather for those to whom the opportunity of managing a house does not come, and for those few who from temperament are more inclined to a life in the open. They should be careful to do tuthing to make harder the lot of their sisters.

oTiae of the causes of the mischief of which town fo„dren. sent into the country villages for their "night's holiday by various charitable agencies .1 8 sometimes accused—the chief cause, perhaps— their. inability at once to take a place in the restful life of the fields. They come out of the noise and stir of narrow streets and courts, and bring with them, in that precocious mind which Can inhabit an antemic body, a sense of feverish activity, which will break out somehow, and in Ways more oir less unlawful, unless some controll- ing influence is at hand. There could be little doubt that all objections raised by farmers and others to the advent of these little strangers wowld soon he removed if only a sufficient number of enlightened People, men and women in sympathy with child- hood, and understanding its ways, could be found, hot to perform the duties of teachers or police,but nimply to set the visitors from townland collecting Wild flowers, or engaging in similar occupations, ih the woods and fields which so soon bring colour te the faded cheeks. Without such a guide the child brought from the heart of townland is simply bewildered, and turns to mischief to escape the Positive sense of oppression which the unexplained face of nature inspires in him. There is evidence enough of this in the answers given by London children to the questions put to them on their re- turn, as set out by Mrs. Barnett in an article in this month's" Nineteenth Century," revealing a Pitiful state of mental confusion with regard to Country objects. This condition of thiegs is clearly remediable, but it is rather for the charitable ancieties concerned in these efforts to consider what is to be done.

It is a matter for congratulation that the corn- nuttee of the House of Lords which considered the aPplication of the Warrington Corporation for a Provisional order to construct tramways within the Borough has taken such a favourable view of .the matter. The Committee decided to pass the 46.111 including the section which was opposed by the London and North Western Railway Company and which proposes to cross the Wilders- Pool level crossing by a tramway. The Only proviso inserted in the Bill is that in the e, vent of the Board of Trade ordering a bridge to be made, the Ce4rporation should contribute half t, be cost. There is no reason to think that a nridge for carrying the tramway over the rail- Way will be required, for there should be no difa- tufty in traversing the level crossing in a tnanner consistent with public safety. The tramway will be able to cross on the ievel quite as easily as an omnibus does at the pre- Sent moment. It would indeed have been the cruellest irony, if after patiently tolerating the railway company's wretched crossing for nearly rty years, the Warrington public had been robbed ef the tramway scheme on its account. Warring- ton has lagged sadly behind other Lancashire towns in means of quick locomotion and we wel- come the dawn of a better state of things.

In the course of a few days the Warrington -v-lectric Lighting Order will come before a corn- kat 30 of the House of Lords. Indications are tot wanting that a certain amount of opposition ?ill be mot with. Locally, opposition was organ- ltied by several firms with the object of obtaining Protective clauses and as the protection asked for 18 not of an unreasonable character, an amicable rrangement has been come to so as to avoid a 4.49ng drawn-out battle in committee. The opposi- rigon of the London and North-Western Railway ;,Jernpany is of a more serious nature. Unfor- "lnately in days gone by this wealthy company t'.?•l3 granted permission to have a .level crossing at ilderspool Causeway and ever since Warrington 44s paid- dearly for this concession in the incom- oding of vehicular traffic and the annoyance and ,Ilpennvenience of foot passengers. But this is not t" 1; We are now face to face with another ditii- wllitY owing to this same level crossing. The rail- ayt company have gn objection to it being j?aversed by an electric tramway, and how the „vrPoration's scheme is to be carried through vithont doing so it is difficult to see. We hope .t the scheme will not be wrecked simply to unge the railway company.

then we come to think of it the impudence of Pa l e London and North-Western Railway Corn- is really colosaal. Never was insult more

deliberately added to injury than in this case of the railway company objecting to the Corporation of Warrington taking their tramway over the level crossing at Wilderspool. That level cross- ing itself is only there on sufferance, it ought never to have been allowed, and we further believe .its days are numbered. Possibly this is the opinion of the railway com- pany . who ilaagine, perhaps, that if they make themselves awkward enough the Corporation will be glad to bridge the crossing, do away with it and clear the route for the tramways. Vain hope! The Corporation is not quitA so foolish as to do something for th 9 railway company which they themselves can be compelled to do, and which would moreoverbe in their own interests.

The Watch Committee have taken a wise step in providing a horse ambulance for Warrington. The need of such a valuable addition to our appli- ances for the conveyance of injured persons to the hospitals has been illustrated on more than one occasion recently. A cab with all its disadvantages of shape is very inconvenient for the purpose, and this is especially the case when it is necessary to lift the patients. Even a cab, too, is not always obtainable and the slower method of carrying by stretchers is often resorted to. Apart from the heavy labour involved the delay occasion9d causes untold pain to the injured person. But with the provision of a horse ambulance under the direction of a capable and energetic police force a better state of things may be looked for. Within call by telephone at any hour of the day or night and with officers always on the spot, as little delay as possible should now be caused in the conveyance of persons who may have the misfortune to need an ambulance.

MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

There is a good deal of electioneering going on in some parts of the provinces, in a quiet way. Can- didates are offering thenaselves,or being asked to do so, and are being asked to attend meetings and state their views. Many are called and a few chosen, and some interesting gatherings are reported. From what is now transpiring it appears that,ceme when the Dissolution may, some of the elections will be very keenly contested. It is much re- gretted, however, that the electoral machinery promises to be in the same unsatisfactory con :ition it was at the last General Election,ween candidates on both sides deplored the state of the register and the practical disfranchisement of many persons who should have been in a position to record their votes. The length of the qualifying period was also felt to be a grievance, and Ministerial candi- dates, as well 88 their political opponents, urged that the period should be shorter. It has been un- derstood all along that although precedence had to be given to more pressing business, electoral re- form would certainty be dealt with before another appeal was made to the country. It is toe late now to pass a comprehensive measure, if Ministers in- tend to take advantage of the patriotic wave that is passing over the country, and any pledges on the subject must be relegated to the limbo of good in- tentions. At the same time, it is felt that there is no reason why a short Act might not be framed to cause the Gensral Election to take place on the new register, and there is no reason, of course,why pres- sure brought to bear upon the Government to this end should COM 9 only from one side of the House. Both political parties experience unnecessary in- convenience when compelled to light an electoral battle with obsolete weapons, and as the chief aim of all should be to got the true verdict of the elec- torate, the contest really ought to take place on the new register. For some time there has been a movement on foot in favour of the registration of music teachers, and to- promote this object, a number of meetingsf are to be held in the provinces. The scheme oa registration is as yet somewhat vague, but the idee is to obtain a list of all music teachers who have passed examinations qualifying them to undertake the task,and to admit none who have not the status of the fully-qualified music-teacher. Possibly the scheme may yet start in a less ambitious way, in the hope that ultimately there may be a kind of corporation of music teachers of the professional class, which will stamp out the amateur. I( the registration of music teachers puts an end to the innumerable class of excellent persons, including bad musicians, who set up schools of instruction in their own homes, and make a street uncomfortable by discordant sounds, and our nights hideous, the promoters will prove "benefactors of their species." The amateur who takes in pupils to bang and practice upon the piano may have as much right as neighbours, to live and earn an" honest shil• ling," but at any rate the neighbours have a right to live also. Ilou,eholders generally will give the registration movement much support if it be made a condition teat music teachers shall live in de- tached houses. There is no disposition to he hard on such teachers generelly ; only upon those, for instance, who are under ne necessity to teach and yet are so hard on others, "Railway Reform" was at one time a standing headline in certain newspaper offices, and the ques- tion was agitated with some effect. Reforms were brought about ef a very useful and substantial char- acter, without any prejudicial effecc upon the com- panies' dividends, and all appeared to be satiefied. Last year the companies almost went out of their way, and they allege now that it was " out of their way" in the most inaporant sense, the pecuniary, to collect and deliver paesengers' luggage at a low rate, and this kind of traffic increased rapidly. But now the rate is abolished, or doubled rathei, and, not content with this kind of volt-face they have raised the rate of excursion fares, and taken to weighing any luggage the passenger may be carrying with him, with an eye upon the possibility of its being heavier than the regulation allows. Thus for any suspicious looking bag the passenger may have with him he is at the tender mercy of the railway porter. They have given due notice of the innovation by posters affixed to the station walls, and that is perfectly fair. But in practice it is giving some trouble to eassengers, many of whom know nothing of the weight of luggage allowed to go free, and complaints are common. The only person who seems pleased with the change is the railway porter, and the reason why it is not necessary to mention. A question was to be asked. baring indirectly upon this and kindred points by Sir Cameron Gull in the House of Commons, and by-and-bye, if things go on as at present, the question of the State con- trol of railways may be revived. Meanwhile, some of the companies have returneri to the practice of plastering the station walls with notices of con- victions obtained against persons for travelling without tickets, in which the name in full, address and amount of penalty imposed by the magistrates, are all boldly set forth, as was the custom of yore. hut for awhile discontinued and understood to he illegal. Whether legal or not, it appears somewhat un-English. There is a great deal of duff e•ence be- tween this kind of publication and newspaper re- ports. as any one of ordinary intelligence may with a moment's censideration plainly perceive. The high pries of coal is mentioned as one of the causes of the present depression in the Lancashire cotton trade. It, may he in this case, as in many others, that the late and present hiah prices are mere excuses for certain people putting money in their purses which otherwise they would have no Plea for keeping, or converting to their own us. There can be no doubt that the price of coal is be- coming and will continue to hecome,an increasingly serious matter in the future. But that is not a reason why the prices should be augmented on every kind of flimsy pretence and for all so-es of sinister purposes by those capable of indulging in them, to their own advancement, end for th,,,ir per- sonal gain. Because we have resigned ourselves t ) having the poor always with us, every ne'er do well thinks AVA Must COMA to his relief also; and an- parently, because coal has become dear, and agents are receiving more money preportionately in the way of cotnrniesion, the prices must he maintained or sent up higher still. Where this cannot be dope, there are other ways of p-ofiting by making present prices the excuse fer indulging in ways that are devious, like the freathen Chinee. Wholesile dealers are " yearnine " the public that prices will be higher in the ensuing winter than ever before, and disinterestedly advising them, therefore, to " give their orders now." There is a strong feeling in the provinces that the Government should make greater provision for the widows and orphans of our soldiers and ssilots. It is not thought creditable to the neion that so many men who have fought, the battles of the 'country in the past should be found in the work- houses as appears to be the case, It may t sbe that there are many inmates of these houses ready to play off "the old soldier" upon unsuspecting visitors who never, were in battle, if, indeed, they were

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT].

Runcorn Anglers' Association have this week opened their waters at the Old Quay. The fishing there, however, has not been as satisfactory as ex- pected, and to all appearances will remain so until the water is cleared of the great masses of weed with which it is filled. On Wednesday there was the first fishing contest of the season. It was highly successful too, but owing to the inclemency of the weather the "sport "was anything but lively.

The prevailing opinion is that the general elec• tion will not come oefore next year; but whether it comes soon or late the quesion of contesting the seat is receiving attention from the Conservative section of the Norchwich Division. There is of course no c_indidate mentioned as yet, but it is common knowledge that the " Constitution " is desirous of obtaining a stroug one, in the hops of wresting the seat from Sir John Brunner. Some of the Runcorn Tories think the opportunity a golden one, and seem inclined to think that Sir John's armour is vulnerable on the " War question." Runcorn Liberals, however, are reciay and prepared to sup- port their present member and do not forget all the town owes to Sir John Brunner, as well as the great amount of good he has done ia the division. Sir John has been an ideal member and ever since be has represented the division,it has been well re- presented. lie will have to be a strong candidate indeed who will disturb the deep-seated regard of the Northwich Division electors for their present servant and legislator --Sir John Brunner.

"Are our city fathers asleep, or have they gone for a walk ?" Are they devoid of the seven senses : or having the full faculties of human beings do they adopt the policy of disregarding the dictates of con- science? Will they kindly drop their supine indifference, and give attention to tho matters indicated as urgent or " by the powers " that rule their destinies—the ever turbulent ratepayers— there shall follow disclosures of a nature that will cause some" sitting up."

This para Japli might cause alarm, and some of the members of our C.RILICiI will naturally begin to ruminate as to what the nature of tie discieure3 might be. rAet their conscience be the judge. Even in their last meeting do not they stand self condemned. Mr. S. Taylor, referring to the iraprovenint of the Rook S'avage-road says : "We are only doing to-day what we should have done 39 years ago !" True, enough, bat ara our Councillors always going to be 30 years behind the times, and that too, on their own confession. Verily Mx. Taylor's words should be hung up in the Coun- cil Cnamber as a text. a motto, a reminder, and used in every speech, as applicable to every tranzi- action. Repairing that road was not the only thing that was required 30 years ago.

Another step towards the completion of Run- corn's Park and I?e!creation Ground was taken yesterday when the Council determined to spend £l,OOO in levelling and other necessary work if the °Ammon is to perform even in a small part the purposes for weich it is intended. There were four dissentients to this needed expenditure, and the chief was ilr U. Bisbrown, who presented the hostile attitude he has ever maintained since the inceptien of the scheme. His admirers may described this as con- sistency; others would use a stronger word of ler different meaning. .Nfr. Bisbrovvn and those who acted with him claim to be voicing the opinions of the ratepapers. How these gentlemen have acquired their knowledge of the views of the ratepayers was not made clear, but those of us, who are not in the inner circle, have had at least two opportunities of gauging the feelings of the people upon this park question. This question was the main plank in the platform of the self styled Ratepayers' Association which passed like a cloud across the horizon of Runcorn to expire after a brief existence, ignominously at a bye-election. This Association held three meetings and yet with all the indignation with which we are told the ratepayers view this park scheme, and which was an unfailing topic for speakers to dilate upon, these meetings did not attract a c4raplee hundred of people. They brought forth a candi- date—he thundered against this so called unrighte- ous expenditure of public money—canvassed and fumed, and when the poll was declared the canAi- date was not scores of votes, but hundreds of votes in arrear. This election was a better voicing of the views of the ratepayers than any given by Messrs. Biebrown and Co. To talk of calling a ratepayer's meeting to decide whether further money should be spent or not is futile. The reso- lution of such a meeting would not be conclusive. If these members have tne courage of their convic- tions, why do they not boldly demand that a proper poll of the inhabitants be taken? Until that is done, in my opinion the Council are acting within their rights, and acting wisely in proceeding to convert a desert into a smiling plain which wiil be a joy to young and old.

The Council have yet power to spend £l.B )0 Mr Taylor, however, is willing that this amount should not be drawn from the rates. Lie favours the idea of a public subscription and with his usual large- hearted generosity is prepared to head the list, and Runcom people from pastexperience know what that means. But he is prepared to go a step further ; if need be ho would go out and beg. This is his own un- varnished statement, and it received acceptance from most members of the Council in the spin- in which it was given, What, however, was the attitude of iniE of the members who was wishful to save the ratepayers, as he said, heavy expenditure upon the common land ? His expression was brief and to the point. It was; "I shall do nothing of the kind !" Men are to be judge by" acti ns not words" Mr. Taylor propo,e I and advocated the spending of the ELMO but at the same time he is willing to give of his substance and of his valuable time, to make the Common" a thing of beauty and a jay for ever,"and doubtless there are many others who will willingly follow his lead. If the four members who vesr,er- day morning prided themselves upon having the courage of their convictions, do no act likewise,the ratepayers will quickly discover that " Cecilia is their friend, not Short."

The police have received information of the som .what sudden death of Edward Turner, a farm labourer. aged 56 years, at no fixed residence,which took place in a stable belonging to Mr. William Brocklebank, landlord of the Black Horse Inn, Birchtield.road, ‘Vidnes, on Scut-day afternoon. The deceased had been in the district for 17 years, and had earned his livelihood by thatching cottages and doing odd work for different people. Lie suffered from chronic bronebitis,and soma time ago was an inmate of the Prescot Workhouse, where he was attended by the doctor for that complaint. He had not been medically attended since that time. About one o'clock on Saturday Mr. Brocklebank found him in his stable. Noticing that he looked very ill ha at once went for Dr. MacLennan,butthat gentle- man was away from home. On his way back he met Dr. Edwards, who went to the stable aril found that the man was dead. Information was despatched to tbe Coroner, but he did not consider an inquest necessary.

TH-E NAVIGATION OF THE MERSEY, LOCAL DREDGINGS. A report "On the present state of the navigation on the river Mersey (1899), to the Right Rout. the Commissioners for the Conservancy of the river," has been prepared by Vice-Admiral Sir G. S. Nares, F R.S., acting conservator of the Mersey. Alluding to the Ship Canal, the report says :—" In ins tidal portion of the canal between Eastharn and Latchford, 476,898 tons of silt were dredged. Of this quantity 193,600 tons were taken to sea, and 283,298 tons were deposited on land belonging to the company beyond the reach of tidal influence. From the length of the canal between Latchford Locks and the Mersey Weir, 40,480 tons of silt and 440 tons of clay were dredged and deposited at sea. In addition •to the foregoing quan- tities, dredging for construction and other purposes was carried out at the mouth of the River Weaver and at Wig works, Runcorn; the total quantity being 31,967 tons, and the material consilting mainly of clay and sandstone rock debris. Of this quantity 18,920 tons were deposited at sea, and 13.087 tons on land above the influence of the highest tides. No new works of special im- portance have been undertaken in the tidal section of the canal during the past yell.. In the lergth of the River NI ersey between\the western boundary of Messrs. Monks. Hall and Company's works at Bank aay, Warrington, and Howley Weir, 149.776 tons of material have been dredged and 4lepositen on land above the influence of the highest Wee' Among the new works for which plans had been submitted to the Conservancy ani approved may be mentioned the alteration on frontage at Bank Quay on the 30th June; the widening of the 14 & N.-W. Railway bridge across the Mersey at. Arpley, on the 9th October, and the extention of for retention of temporary jetty at Ogle Beach, by Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co.. 6th November. " Mary Ellen " sometime betw,en 6 p.m. on the 27th June, and 6 a.m. on the 28. h of .Tune.—The rope was the ptonery of Mr. C. V, H Harrison. of Runcorn.—Enneh Hulse, Captain of the flat, sgid on the 27th ult., his flat, was lying at th. Old goay, Runcorn. When he left her about. 6 o'clock on Wednesday evening the rope was lying on the deck. The following morning the rope was missing, and be gave information to the police. During the period mentioned the ivisonere boats were also lying at the Ole) Quay. —P C. George Car- rington, as the result of investigation, found the prisoners'boats lying in the Bridgewater Canal near the Halton-road Crane. He boarded them and found the rope (produced) on hoard. He took the prisoners into custody. and in answer to the charge the elder one said " I know nothing about the thin piece of rope." I don't know how that, came on board, but the thick piece was picked up by my son Bill out of the Canal at Northwich." The son said " I don't know anything about them ; some• body must have thrown them upon the boat. 4 Afterwards when in the cell the son supplemented his remarks by saying "I took them, my father didn't."—The elder prisoner elected to give evidence. and swore he had nothing to do with the theft., but in cross-examination, by Superintendent 1.) Pearson, he admitted there was a large quantity of coal and other materials in his house for which he could render no account.—Both prisoners were sentenced to 21 days' hard labour without option.

A CANDID A.TE FOE THE HIIMA.NE SOCIETY MEDAL.

A number of people witnessed a very plucky rescue from drowning in the Ship Canal, at Run- corn on Saturday afternoon, A boy named Mere- dith Ivens, 11 years ot age, residing in Cooper- street, Runcorn, was "crabbing" in company with a nnmber of otuer lads off the landing-stage, by the Ferry Hut, when he overbalanced himself and felt into the water. Captain Harvey, who resides in South Bank-terrace, Runcorn, heard the cries of the boy's cJmpanions for help, and on going outside he took the situation in at a glance. He quickly scrambled down the wall, and ran to the canal side. Without divesting himself of his clothing, which was very heavy tun had just come off duty at tie docks), he plunged in, and succeeded in bringing the boy safely to toe side. The boy was able to walk home with the assistance of some friends. This is not the first instance in which Captain Har- vey has saved life, and it is to. be hoped that this act will be brought under the notice of the Royal Humane Society. Captain Harvey's brother was going down the canal at the time in the Pomona, and was a witness of the gallant act of his brother, Captain Harvey Was very much exhausted when he got ashore again.

INACCURICIE3 AND FILSE INTPRESSIONIq,

SOME REFLECTION. UPON THE. REjEN

STRIFE

To the Editor of the Runcorn Examinee—Dear sir.— Will you kindly permit inn on behalf of myself ani col- leagues as r'presenting the workers at the Runcorn Dock to expreaa our thanks to the press for their valuable assistance rendered to our cause and through the Peeas to the local authority and the people of Runcorn for their manifest sympathy with us in nor recent trouble, With your permission we desire to point ow, a few inaccuracies end false impressions, without tauehing upon debatable matter connected with the dispute.T ;ere was not a atreke as generally underatood. We hid "übmitted a claim for advanced rates for the considera- tion of the company, and beyond this we had no immediate intention of applying pressure upon the Canal Company. A suceesaion of events created by the company foro.ed the conflict and we are more than satisfied be its results. The importation ot a consider able force of policemen, gives the impression of Remo kind of panic in the mind of our loot! au hority. Was this force necessary and who were they to protect ? The evolution of events has proved they were unnecessary. We had no qua-rel with the people of Runcorn, in fact we found no inconsiderable rovion of the people and our interests were Lientical. The narrow-boatmen did not require protection frem ea A moment's calm consideration will show that these men whose action precipitated the crisis were really w ,rkine our own emancipation. Pressure applied by their own nifficultiea—by their employers—by maniilncture,s— -compelled them to take up a position which we knew must bring a solution. Surely, it was not expected 'hat the Shipping Federation men were in danger. If so then it proves that the lesaions of the toast hive been unheeded by our local rulers. A mere handful of men instructed by their employers unaccustomed to the work and although reef iyine good wages incapable of handling the cargoes on profitable terms and hearing a name that is a. brewerd—these men were rather objects of commiseration than hostility. The most powerful weapon in the hands of trades unionists is order, and ehis we endeavoured with all our influence to miintain and it is admitted cur efforts were auccesifol. We are sometimes t meted with driving trade free.' the pa-t. It would be nearer the truth to say that the label?. troubles are not created be us and that we have always been the victims of a vicious system that ignores the reasonable claims of the W'Tklr3 as represented by intelligent unieniam. We find the fool for our wives and families around these docks and it is our interest to encourage such trade se long as it admits of a fur return for our labours. Trielefore our interests are identical with those of the town. We cannot how- ever even in the interest of trade be expected to de- generate into galley slaves. Another erroneous imerea- sion is that; Trad. Unionists are always bursting for a fight. To our mind Unionism is deficient when it fai's to avert senikes. We however are not so conceited as to think that we could hope to defeat a peweinul company backed by a still more pewerful corporation,, unlesa our claims as those put forward recently were such as to commend thernaelvn to tee reasonable consideration of the Canal Company anti the public generally. The policy of force is past and reason has entered into the arena of social conflicts. Aeitin thanking you, WA remtie, yours truly, Joseph Billam (president), Ellis Gatley (secretary).flrener Mersey Watermen and Porters- Association, Jule 3rd, 1900. - THE PARK AND CODISION. The Highways and Building Committee recommended that a sum of £I,OOO be expended on the Common on had drainage, tevelling,manuring and planting,pitching , sides of lake, and making of footpaths. Mr. S. Taylor, in moving the recommendation of the Highways and Buildings Committee, pointed out that in addition to the £I,OOO asked for that morning, to be expended an draining, eta., the Council had also sprit the sum of about £1.200 in making the road from the Heath to the boundary of the township by Rack Savage. In his opinion, however, this £1,200 termed no part of the Common scheme at all. It had been expended in the making of a road that ought to have been made long ago. There had been general complaint; from every- body that the road was neither fit for carts nor wheel- , barrows or vehicles of any description. The £l,OOO , asked for that morning would be the best spent money , the Council bad ever spent in Runcorn. There was no recreation ground for the town of 20,000 inhabitants. It was a scandal that the only recreation ground for the young people was the puolic streets, with the result that many children had been crippled for life,snd that almost week after week with distressing frequency firm beal.hy growing lads had been brought before the magistrates for playing football and other games in the streets. He felt sure that when this £l,OOO was spent the young people would have facilities for r.creation which no doubt would be highly appreciated. Pei sonally he would rather have seen Bancorn people adopt the course that Widnes had proceeded on, and have their work done by public subscription. He would have taken his part and would hare been glad to bead the list, but as nothing had been done in this way it was the duty of the Council to provide suitable accomnasdation for the young people. Mr. Wiswall seconded without comment.

Mr. Bisbrown proposed an amendment tbatthe recom- mendation referring to the Common be deleted from the minutes. He was not, he said, individually, opposed to having a park and recreation ground, but from the inception of the scheme he had strongly urged that it was obj?ctioaable on the ground of Cast. Be knew that in voicing his views that morning he was leading a forlorn hope, but he spoke sincerely ; and if there was an election in his I ward to-morrow he would stand or fall by that issue. There were luxuries they would all like to have but they could not afford them and he placed the Common scheme in that category. He should vote against the recommendation on the grouad of excessive expenditure. Mr. arme seconded the amendment and said he was in favour of the issue being placed before the ratepayers, whether they were in favour of the scheme being finished entirely or not. BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

SINGULAR CASE AT WARRINGTON.

At the B irough Police Court, on Monday morning, before Mr. Monks (in the chair) and other magistrates, a serious charge was preferred against Ralph Plant, jun. (who did not appear), of Pit Green-lane, Wolstanton, Staffordshire, namely, that of having on the 6th April "unlawfully and knowingly by a certain false pretence, fraudulently induced William Breward to accept a certain valuable security, to wit, a bill of exchange, for the payment of the sum of £2ll."—Mr. Browne said that a week ago he applied for an adjournment of the case at the request of the defendant wita the idea that be would endeavour to settle the matter, this not being a felony but a misdemeanour. He convnted,and be had an offer from tho defendaut in writing, but it was so ridiculous that he (Mr. Browne) bad made no reply to it. On Saturday morning a better offer Was received, but it was also refused, and he told defendant that the case must proceed. This was a very bad case. Mr. Breward was an earthenware dealer, eharying on business in Buttermarket-street. He had recently started business, and evidently the defendant must have known that because he telegraphed to him to ask if he wanted certain things. The reply sent was that if they were cheap the complainant was prepared to buy, and Mr. Plant came over, saw the cemplainent and told him that he had to buy out a partner on the following Saturday and had to find £350, and that if he would buy the crates at Longport station he would take off five per cent. discount, with two bills, one is a month and another in four months. His client asked most particularly if the goods were at Lengport station and he repeatedly relined that they were, and further that they would be consigned to Mr. Brea rd's order as soon as def-ndant got tuck. Thereupon Mr. Plant pro- duced certain invoices and showed him the goods. , Mr. Breward went through them, and as they were what be wanted he bought them, giving two bills of exchange, one for £24 and the other for £3O, one being due in a month ann the other in four months; Finding the com- plainant was a man not used to business the defendant telegraphed to him that--The Chairman : Has he met the bills 9—Mr. B-owne : They are not due yet, but we shall be compelled to meet them. Continuing, Mr Browne said tte defendant informed the complainant that he had more ,toff, but, being susWcioue, Breward got a witness to hear all that was said, which was to the effect that there were more crates lying at the station and that he would take bills—one ror four months and the other for five .months. The total bills amounted to £lll. If the gouds had turned up the matter would have been perfectly satisfactory. The first lot was right, but the second differed entirely, and the third was a ridiculous lot. The complainant wrote to Plant that the crates were not proper owe, and that he must send the right 00, not thinking there was any- thing wrong. He got no reply and went to Longport, when Plant said he had been ill, that the packer had made a mistake, and that he would send in due core. fie wanted to supply other goods but his client, suspect log that something was wrong, went to the station but could not rind a single crate there. Mr. Breward weuld uct have parted with the money if he had not believed the crates were at the station awaiting his order. That was the false pretence coupled with the fact that there was no partner to pay out. The defendant had since become a bankrupt.—Evidence having been given by Mr Breward, Police-Sergeant Williams, of Wolst anton, said he served the defendant with the summons and his reply was that he strou'd not have been surprised to have received a warrant. He added "It was only an ordinary business treesac ion. I cannot 'ielp it. The business has failed. I am not the only one that is in it." —The Chairman: You want a warrant against the defendant 9—Mr. Browne: Yes, but I am afraid he has gone away now.—The warrant was granted.

AN INGENIOUS BEGGING IMPOSTER.

A man named John Walsh was charged at the War rington Police Court on Monday with being an idle and disorderly person and going st,-out as a col'ector and gatherer of alins.—The Chief Constable in asking for a remand for eight days aid that at 830 on Saturday night Superintendent Monaghan was in the Market Place and noticed the prisoner exhibiting the following notice on the front of his chest Kind friends, I am a cotton operative throws out of employment through a mill fire that occurred at Oldham a short time since. I am a widower with four little children." The Superintendent had suepicions and t)id an offic •r to caution him. He again saw the min at 10-30 in Horeemarket-street. Be had his left band tighly bandaged with volumin ms cloths. The Superintendent spoke to the man, and the prisoner no kdowing to whom he was talking, said he was suffering from blood poisoning. He bad a rusty pin run into his hand and could not work; He wanted a few elvers to pay frr lodgings for himself and chi'd,en. The Superinteudent intended to give him a night's lodgings, so he Amought him te the Policl Station. The sum of 7s. 111. was found inebis trousers pockets. The Superintendent removed the handkerchief from the hand and found that the sore place did not exist and the hand was 89 sound as his own. He gave the name of Walsh and said he resided at a house in West-street, Oldham. WARRINGTON'S TRIP DAY. Messrs. Richmond's Employes at Douglas.

The employes of Messrs. Richmond and Co., Limited, together with their wives and friends had a long day of pleasure last Saturday on the occasion of the annual excursion. That most popular of resorts—Douglas—was the place visited, and the outing proved to be one of the most successful ever experienced in the history of the firm. Admirable arrangements had been made and the event passed off without the slightest hitch. At the early hour of five o'clock the platform at the Central Station was thronged by upwards of a thousand trippers bent on making the most of the opportunity ot enjoying themselves to the full. Three special trains conveyed them to Liverpool which was reached at 5.30. When the excursionists arrived a a peaceful calm was hovering over the City, soon to be broken by the hum and bustle of business. The firm had engaged toe services of the Borough Band and the members marched from the station to the Prince's Land. tog Stage playing lively airs. The splendid steamship ef the Ile of Man Steam Packet Company, "The Prince' of Wales" was here in waiting. The boat is one of the fastest paddle steamers afloat travelling at the rate of twenty-three miles an hour. The trippers quickly boarded her, and as the boat left the Landing Stage and I I headed her bows for Douglas the band struck up "Soldiers of the Queen." Up to now the weather con- ditions had not been very, promising and soon a drizzling 'rain commenced but fortunately did HOC last long. After crossing the Bar a stiff breeze sprang up and the sea lb( came rather" choppy." The spray dashed over the side of the boat and many of those passengers who were travelling in the bow of the vessel were drenched. In a very short space of time many of the trippers, were hors de combat. The journey was accomplished in about four hours and alter the somewhat rough passage I no one was sorry when the rugged coast line of Mona's Isle, backed by ii:bly wooded slopes, came into view. When the pleasure seekers arrived at Douglas the climatic conditions ha I urdergone a completi :change for the better. The sun shone brightly and the blue sky was almost cloudtess. The majority of the trippers dispersed to spend the day as they pleased whilst the directors and a number of invited guests repaired to the Villiers Hotel where char-a-basics were in waiting to con- vey.them across the island to Part Erin. The country is now wearing its most charming aspect and the drive was a delightful experience. The route taken lay through splendid scenery and the drive occupied about three hours. It was two o'clock when the party arrived at Port Erin and they at once proceeded to the Belle Vue Hotel where a capital dinner was served. Tne long drive had whetted the appetites of the visitors and needless to say they did ample justice to the savoury viands pro- vided. At the conclusion of the meal several short 1 speeches were made. Mr. HirCHMOND presided, and among others present were :—Mrs. Richmond and Mr. Frank Richmond, I Messrs. J. A. Ransome (general manager at Warrington) H. P. Hughes. (cashier), J. F. Davy (works manager at I Warrington),Councillors Smethurst and Plumpton (War- rington), Ccuecillor Andrews (Langley Green), Mr and M s. T. Birtles, Mr. Bickford, Mr. T. Lcngdin, Mr. 'Loft and others. After dinner Mr. RICHMOND announced that be had received three telegrams. Mr. Harold Ranaome wired: " With you in thought, and hope all will have an enjoyable day and continued success." Miss Lillie Richmond telegraphed. " Wish you all a j )lly day," and the Stratford works sent the fillowing message: "Stratford hopes you will , have a glorious day and much enjoyment. —THORNTON." Mr. Richmond Said he was sure they were all much ' obliged for the sympathetic messages, and they hoped to return the compliment to Stratford when their outing took place on the following week.; Mr. RICHMOND submitted the toast of the Queen, and it having been duly honoured, Mr. J A RANSOMS proposed a vote of thanks to Mr. and Mrs. Ricneaoad and Master Frank Richmond for their presence. Tney were, be said. always very pleased to see them. That was the first annual excursion he (Mr. Ranaome) had attended, and he bad enjoyed him- self very much, as he believed thay all had. He had pleasure in thanking Mr. and Mrs. Richmond and family for their presence as trial gathering. (Applause.) The proposition was carried with enthusiasm- Mr. RICHMOND, in acknowledging the compliment, said they were very pleased to be present and meet the members of the staff in that way. His own convictioa was—and, it was founded upon ROM experience—that when one could mix with his staff on terms of freedom and without that restraint which ousiness naturally imp ised upon them, there was a cordial feeling at once manifested which had very marked effects upon the pro- gress of the business. (Hear, hear). The cordial ieang whith existed between them and him was the reason, he believed, of the marked success and progress of the firm. They all knew he was very pleased to have their corn- ' pally. They were once more in the proud position of being able to say, as they said every year, that an increase had berm made on the previous year's business. (Hear, hoar) Last year although they had a big increase he had to say that Stratford had a still bigger increase, but this year Warrington had pulled its socks up— (laughter)—and had established a record. 'The business to the and of May showed an increase of 58i per ceat. over 1899. (Applause). Although the business was now iof such magnitude ttiere were very few complaints, and he could oaly exeress his great appreciatioe of the enthusiasm and earnestness with which the members of the staff worked together. With such conditions nothing was impossible ; acid he looked forward to them achiev- inh still better results in the future. (Applause). Mr. RICHMOND then proposed the toast of the office and works staffs and the travel'ers. Messrs. RANSOMS, HUGHES, Davy and MILLS briefly responded. The toast of" The Visitors" was drunk. Councillor SmersußsT, in responding, said they had had a very pleasant gathering. He was glad to hear that the Warrington works were doing so well. Nothing succeeded like success. Whilst on the journey to Port Erin he noticsd such words as " Rushtorth," "Rich- mood," " Success " and" Lancashire" on the brakes and hotels. Be would put them together and give t.ern a motto from them to read: " Rush forth Richmonds to success and Lancashire." (Laughter and applause.) Councillor ANDREWS also responded and the interesting proceedings terminated. Rain now commenced to fall s) that the drive tack to Douglas was abandoned and the party went by train. After spending about two hours in Douglas the return journey was commenced, Liverpool being reached after an agreeable voyage at a quarter to eleven. At midnight the trippers left the Central Station and arrived in War- rington at 12.30 feeling tired but nevertheless agreed that this year's excursion was about the most enjoyable that has yet been held.

Aberystwyth was the place ,elected for the annual outing of the emplcyea of Mr. R. A. Naylor, timber mer- chant, Howley. The qi,cial train, which contained be- tween 300 and 400 emplord;, wives, sweethearts and friends, was timed to leave Bank Quay Station at five o'clock on Saturday morning, and a few minutes after that hour it started on its journey. A. saloon had been provided for the heads of the various departments. Mr L Henshall, the works manager, had made admirable arrangements to ensure the pleasure and comfort of the triprers, light refreshments being distributed at one or two stooping places en route for all in the train. Rain fell during the early part of the journey, but long before the train reachei its destination the sun made its appear- ance, and not a cloud was to be seen. The route taken was throueh Crewe, Whitchurch, Welsbampton, Oswestry, Welsbpo 1, Glandovey, and Borth, and the train steamed into the station at Aberystwyth at 10 30, or exactly an hour after the advertised time. Although the journey was a long one it was by no means tedious owing to the charming scenery on both sides of the railway. On arrival at Aberystwyth,the excursionists dispersed to spend the day as pleased them best. Toe heads of the departments end a few friends, altogether to the num- ber of about 40, repaired for breakfast to the Belle Vue Hotel. A capital breakfast had been prepared. (Hear,bear,and applause.) That was the first time the employe; had visited that picturesque part oi the world. They were all pleased with the journey and their beautilul surroundings, and

he hoped they would return greatly benefited by the change and thus be the better able to fellow their employment. (Applause.) Mr. HENSHALL also respinided. Mr. W. DAINTITH prop9sed success to the firm." _ _ _

During the day visit i were paid to the various objec!s of interest in the town and district. Rain fell heavily during part of the afternoon, and most of the visitors from Warrington and elsewhere found the entertainment an agreeable way of spending a couple of hours. The caves and the town were also inspuc'ed. GOSSAGE'S ANNUAL TRIP.

The workpeople emplr.ye I by the well-known firm of Messrs William Gos3age and Sons, Limited had their annual excursion last Saturday. Windermere, that beautiful resort situate in the charming Lake District, was tt e rendezvous selected, and the outing was looke forward to by all with pleasurable anticipation. With their cuAtomary munificence MEsirs Gos3age had allowed the bonus of 13 61 Cr, the men and is to the boys em- ployed by them, and there is no doubt that this would prove very useful in ass'sting the expenses. The em- ploye;,numbering atout I,Boo,departed from:the Widnes London and North-Western Railway Company's station by three special trains at 6 a.m., 6.15. and 6.40. At that time the elemonts did not present a very favourable appearance,and grave doubts were created as to whether the" trippers" would be favoured with that fine weather which is such an important factor to real enjoyment. After Wigan h3d been left behind the atmospheric con- ..

ditions vre greatly imoroved, and beyond an occa- sional gloom nothing further occurred during the day to ' damp the spirits of the excursionists. Windermere was reached be all first train at about nine o'clock, the secend at 9-30, whilst the last only arrived at about 11-25. Upon alighting, the peoWe diseersod in all direetione. The majority strolled down to the lake and watched the pleasure boats or took a trip on one of the small steamers to Ambleside. a place of great beauty some dig ince along the lake. These who availed them- selves of the latter were fully recompensed. The scene from the lake is one of the finest one might cver wish to see. The valleye are densely wooded, with hero and there a gentleman's residence or the more humble or quaint abode of the cottager. The hage mountains or fells, which towered in tne background, looked inex- pressibly grand, veiled as they were intermittently in a slight mist. Another method of enjoyment was to cross the lake by the steam ferry and scale the fells which are on the other side. Considemble pleasure could be derived from this, as an excellent view was obtainable of the lake for several miles. In the after- noon a yacht race took place, and great interest was manifested in the course which the " white winged " craft adopted. The business eatnblishmenta in the town were liberally patroniz ad by the visitore, who were anxious to take home some necaneete of an ir outing. As six o'clock approached they wended their way to the railway station,and by 6 40 the trains with their frieght of tired people were on their return journey. The first train arrived back at about nine o'clock. the second half-an-heur later, aod the third about 9.35. Every- thing passed off most suecesstully, and a very delightful day was spent. Toe general arrangements, which were parried out by Mr. Povah in a most efficient and praise- worthy manner, were perfect. Mr. McConnell, the stationmaster, accompanied the excursion, and ably superintended the railway arrangements.

_—•_—• New Stationmaster for Warrington. _

Mr. H Aspinall, the night stationmajer at Pres- ton, has been appointed the stationmaster on the London and North Western Railway system at Warrinvton in succession to Mr. Brookshaw, who has retired.

Local Works Under the Hammar.

At the Lion Hotel, on Wednesday evmniug, Messrs Thomas Sutton and Sons offered for sale by auction the works in Thvne-street, lately in the occupation of Messrs. Fletcher, Russell and Company, Limited, gas apparatus manufacturers, &c. The premises cover an area of 5,075 square yards and can be adapted for engineering works, cycle works, brass foundry, cabinet works. builders' works or for belting manufactory. No satisfactory effer was made and the works were withdrawn.—Messrs Davies atid Forshaw were the solicitors.—On the same evening Mr. William Hickman offered for sale by auction at the Blue Ball Inn, Horsemarket-st., the Longford-street iron and steel works. There was a fair attendance. Bidding started at £350 and reached £410; but as the reserve price of £450 was not reached, the lot was withdrawn.—Mr Arthur J Willett, of Lyme-street, was the solicitor for the sale.

STOCKTON HEATH.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A CRIMEAN VETERAN.—About 5.30 on Stturday morning, an army pensioner. named Samuel B:ackshaw Hewitt, aged 72, who resided with 1115 nephew and niece, at 1, Leonard-street, Stockton Heath - was found dead on his bedroom floor. He had been Connected With the 77th Regiment and had been through the Crimean War (in whiqh he was slightly wounded) and the Indian Mutiny. He had been in a feeble norl- dition for some time past, being thorough!,, worn out. He had a serious illness in October, and suffered from a weak teart. He had lately been troutikd with a cough —The icquest was held at the Mu berry Tree Inn, ort oefore Mr. E. A. White, deputy coroner, when a verdict of " Death from natural causes " was returned

Religious Liberty and Courtesy.

To lirv. F E Powell.—Dear Sir,—l have no descre whatever to give you the opportunity, you so much covet of posirg as a martyr. I simply pointed out to YOU, that if I had done in your graveyard what you have done in our--set at defiance the letter and spirit Of the Act of 1880—I arc uld have been guilty of a mis- demeanour. Y, u ought to have respected that act as much as I am expected to do. What is sauce fcr the goose, is saute for the gander. Ycur scream, to "fetch a policeman r at the end of your last letter, shows that you are quite incapable of defending your position, and is as undignified as your conduct all along on this matter has been unkind and discourteous. You are evideqtly ignorant of the fact that Baptists are never given to prosecute or persscute those sr ho differ from them in faith and practice—for their _faith and practice—but they do believe that they have a perfect right to protest against the minister of any church, who needlessly and offensively parades his own distinctive ritualistic propaganda, on their property and so abusing a privilege ; and it is such a protest I have made. JAMES SAMUEL HUGHES, 20, eliffe Tew, Stockton Heath, June 2811,1900.

Warrington Rural District Council and Padgate Footpaths.

Si.,—" Co-utinuine be (Mr. Thornton) said he was Sorry that thi, Special Committee and the Council gener- ally bad dealt with the Wesleyan body as they had done."

_ This remark is included in your report of the last meeting of the above-mentioned Council at which I regret I was not present,as I would have said then' what, by your permission and usual courtesy, I will shortly say here, which is that Mr. Thornton knew perfectly well in common with every other.memb"r of the Council that the question had practically been previously dealt with by the Council, the explanation being that the Connty Council declined,to bear any part of the expense of tiling footpaths in such placrs, hut specially gave consent to do so in the case of that part alone the frontage of Padgate Day School in, consideration of the purchase of land for the purpose of widening the foot- path and the extra wear at that place. If that were not PO and Mr. Thornton feels this sym- pathy for the Wesleyan body which he professes to do why did he not call the attention of the committee to the matter when on their visit of inspection? They met Lim by appointment at an early hour of the day and although he accompanied them over almost every part of the Parish be never in any way alluded to the matter and still he will now make it a handle to cast aspersions on the Committee and Council generally._

I apllogise for troubling you, and beg to remain, yours truly,

WM. HOUGHTON

Paddington, Juno 28th, 1900

FOREIGNERS .SAID TO BE MURDERED AT PEKIN.

British Legation Burned.

iEmperor and Dowager-Empress Reported Poisoned. (112utvr's Telegram.) SHANGHAI. WEDNESDAY. It is statcd on g,ootl authority that t'area Chinese who were in the service of the foreigners in Pekin have escaped from the capital. They report that all foreigners, to the number of 1,000, including 400 soldiers. 100 members of the Chinese Custom staff, and women and ehildren,held out in the British Legation until ammunition and food Were exhausted . A Shanghai message of Tuesday says :—Chineso teports state that not a foreiguer is now alive in Pekin. It was filled with silt and wont:rind and the killrd were lying unburied in heaps b-ith inside and outside. Men of all nationalities were mingled together. VIADUCT WORKS PAINT SHOP PICNIC.

seve;lY.37ai-e w;i:li-rne-n employed in the paint shop of the Viaduct Works, to the number of about 80, had an outiog on Saturday to Billings. Leav- ing the Griffin Hotel in three waggonette, the party had a very enjoyable seven miles drive, making the Causeway Hotel at Billings their heed- quarters. On arrival they were photographed sn the Bowling Green, and a howling handicap was afterwards commenced. After the first round had been completed, however, the handicap had to be abandoned owing to rain. The next item was a pedestrian handicap, which was won by J. Gilman, Tea was afterwards partaken of, and the remainder Of the evening was spent in a sociable manner. A portion of the Viaduct Band accompanied the pie nic, and considerably enlivened the proceedings.

===== 0002601_19000706_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
EARLESTOWN WESLEYAN. CHOIR.

The members of this choir had their annual pic- nic on Saturday. The destination this year was Llangollen. and thither the party proceeded by the 7.13 train from Warrington, a spacial saloon being provided for their accommoeation. Llangollen was 'reached in good time, and dinner wi.s partaken of atll o'clock at the Wymistay Arms. The next item on the programme was a drive during which they passed the Horse Shoe fails. At the end of the drive the party alighted,and sang several piecee afterwards returning to Llangollen. They made point of visiting die residence of the " old maids " at Pins Newydd. after which they returned to the Hotel for tea. The remainder of the time was spent in a voyage up the Canal as far as the Horse Shoe falls where a stay of a few minutes was made. After returning to Llangollen the party wended their way to the station. and resiehed Earlestown soon after 9 30 With the except ipn Of a slight shower tbe weather was all that could be desired and much credit is due to Mr. Uot.-lin for the excellence of the ,arrangemente.

===== 0002601_19000706_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
THE WA

A .Vilarrington Volunteer Dies in Natal.

• The melancholy news reached Warr:ngton on Monday that Private George Sue. low, who went out with the first contingent of Warrington Volunteers, had died in Natal of enteric favor. The first intimation was conveyed in the.following letter which was received by Mrs. Sudlow from •Captain Appleton :—•‘ De Wet's Farm, Botha's Pass, Natal, June 4th, 1900.—You will I know have heard, long ere this reachcs you, the sad news of the death of your poor lad. I only got the telegram yesterday, and I only know that he died from enteric fever at Princess Christian's Hospital at Pine Town,near Durban, where, I presume, he is buried. He was taken ill at Wasenbank Farm on our long I march from Elandslaagte, and of course, ycu lose sight of men at once. You move on and he either remains in the rear in the Brigade Field Hospital or he is sent down country, but I am sure he would te well looked aftcr. Please accept my deepest sympathy, und that of all the officers and men in the company, in your great sorrow. Be was a nice lad, and no one regrets losing him more than I do. You will have the consolation of knowing and feeling that he did a noble thing in volunteering to corns out and fight for his Queen and country for whom be has died. I will try to get told of his field glasses and send or bring them home to you, together with any other little things I thiek you would like, but you will know that things take a long time to do in this huge country; and we are moving further every day." This was the first intimation that the relatives had of the death ef Private Sudlow, his name and number (4,452) not having appeared in the daily published casualty lists, and no information having come from the War Office. His death must have occurred about a month ago. Private Sudlow, was only 21 years of age. He had been a member of the Warrington Volunteers since February 1898 He was a carter, and had been employed at Messrs. Rylands Bros. Works, Church-street, since he left school. Ho is the second Warrington Volunteer who has died in South Africa.

===== 0002601_19000706_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
Return of Colonel Ireland Blackburne.

On Friday evening Colonel Ireland Blackliurne who went out to South Africa in command of the South Lac- cashire Militia, received a warm welcome on his return to Hale. Colonel Blackburne contracted enteric fever in South Africa, and upon his recovery to convalescence, he was invalided home. The gallant gentleman and his wife arrived at Ditton Junction by the London express at 5 45, and as they walked from the station to their carriage they were vociferously cheerei by several hundreds of peonlo who had assembled. The line of route to Hale was lined with cheering spectators. At the bottom of the st,tion bridge an arch was .erested across the roadway, whilst the words "Welcome home," worked on a bright materiil, appeared on several cottages close by. The decorations in Halo were also profuse, inueh time evid- ently having beea expended upon them. On arrival at the hall gates an interesting function took place. This consisted of the pmentatien of a beautifully-illuminated address given by the tenantry to Colonel Blackburne. The duty of handing over the present was fulfilled by Mr. William Turten, Burn Mill Farm, the oldest tenant on the estate, who de)ivered a !eve' complimentary re- marks appropriate to the occasion. Be expressed the great thankfulness that all experienced that the ColQnel had recovered from his illness, and that he was once more in their midst. Colonel Blackburne briefly responded, thanking all for their thoughtfulness and kindness. The horses were then taken from the carriage, and ropes being at hand, it was dragged down the drive to the hall door by several of the tenantry. Here Colonel and Mrs. Blackburn° shook bands pith several old friends, and the happy proceedings terminated.

Runcorn Men at the Front. A Good Word for Buller.

Private G. Johnson, of the D Company, 2nd Devon Regiment, writing to his brother, Mr. Ali xander John son, tailor, 29, Thomas-street, Runcorn, writes :—" I daresay you have read of the great suceess we have had on this, side, which has been gained by hard marching. We made our advance on the 9:h of the month, our object being to take the Biggarsonrg—or as the Boers have named it—the Gibralter of Snath Africa. We have to thank our commander (General Buller) for the way in which his troops worked, his plan causing us to really outflank the Boers. It was drawing towards evenieg when we climbed the last mountain, and I cmn tell you wo had some steep ones to mount that day. We were getting done up, but when we got to tne top of the ridge we espied the Boer position. Our artillery got into position about 4 or 5 o'clock and began shelling them, though tt-e Boers were the first te open fire, ours were soon retaliating and kept it up rill dark when all was silent for the night. Had we attacked the Boers the way they expected us, ther,i is no doubt our losses would have been swore; but owing to our good genera ship—for I can tell you no is the right man in the right place,and he is liked by all under him—we occupied the Boer trenches that night. At daybreak nest morning we expocted a great battle, but te our dismay upan advancing we thugs! the Boers had all retir d, settieg fire to the grass to cover the retirement. Our Cavalry and Horse Artillery gave chase and the latter success- fully shelled the Boers. As we advanced we saw several deal- horses, the result of the shelline, but could not tell what the Boer losses were. Our loss wasl seven wounded. We stayed at Dundee for the nit: tr. Here the Boers had destroyed the property and tn., railway bridges, and we had to make tnees good. We celebrated the Queen's birthday ve.terday by repairing the railway, but it was ao use grumbling as it had to be done. We are at a place celled Newcastle now, and ths Boers have served it the same as Dundee. Tney have , broken the shop windows and looted all they could get hold of. We are getting a big supply of rations here bsfore we advance, so our next move may be a big one. We have got Majuha and Laing's Nek to face next and I hope we shall pull through it. We are all eetting tired of it I can tell von. I don't supnose you will Lae another letter from me Until we reach Pretoria. sVe shall have some B rough fighting, but it cannot last long, for we have a lot c f Boor prisoner in this camp. I had a chat with four of them when I was on guard over them, and they seemed to say they were forced into it but you cannot lo,lieve them When we came into Newcastle they were flying the white flag, pretending to be British subjects, but if I had my way with a lot of them I would imprison them for life or give them " a pill "—you know what that means. I see a lot in the paper criticising Buller ab ,ut Spion KOD. But give Buller his due. He has had all the hard fighting, and, some of those" nobs "at home if they were to go on swanking their i.ipee and mind themselves it would lot k better of them. Buller is all ught, or at least these who are fighti g under him think so. Be suits us all right. Good luck to him!" The la ter concludes with fond remembrances to family and inquiring friends. The same correspondent writing to Mr Ford, Dover- street, Runcorn. says :—" I enquired of the men in the Fouth Lancashire Regiment for the Runcorn Men, and found a few of them. One especially wishes to be re- membered to you, and that is elcConn. Ho knows you well and has given me a bit of a letter to put in for you The Runcorn men happened to come into our camp, hut they are away again now. We have had some hard sh-ps to contend with but I think they will soon come to a close now. We have a party of Boers hemmed in hero. I don't know if they will surrender or have another battle, but it does not matter which way they have it. They are bound to lose. But you know it means the loss of life and bloothh.d for no- thing. Our people may starve them out or shel them out of it, but our General (Buller) is a g-od ma's and doesn't sacrifice his men's lives. Be does everything he can for us, and his men are willing to go and do any, hirer for him. They only want the word to go end they are there. Ic an tell you General Buller has had some hard work to do.I hare teen reading how th,3 criticised him about the Spin!) lion affair. Better they closed their mouths until the war wee over,er corns aeid try to mend things if thee can. Bullet's men do not think he is to blame, and I can tell you they are upset about it. "Tommy Atkins" would go anywhere with him. He worked a fine move wirn we cleared th- Biggarsburge with only seven wounded. lam thankful . to say I have pulled through so far but you know I was woended crossing the Tugela, hut I am vetting all right now. My wound is all right, and I shall ba glad when we return to old England, for I can tell yeu we are all "fed up" with it. We are now where the big battle was fought in 1881 where General Colley lost bis life."

MILITARY NOTES.

After a long delay Captain and 'Honorary Major J. D. Buckton has been formally promoted sub- stantive Major in the hat Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment at Warrington, becoming second in command of the corps in , succession to Major H. J. Widdows who entered at the com- mencement of the year. Major Buckt on has com- manded a company in the battalion since April 1835 and his company command has now gone to Lieu- tenant Bernhard, the senior subaltern in the bat- talion who has been formally promoted Captain. This officer became Second Lieutenant in the corps in March 1897 and was promoted .to the rank of Lieutenant in July of the following year. Major Prendergast, 2nd Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment, who was a few months ago appoin- ted station staff officer at Roorkee, has now been appointed to act as assistant military secretary to Lieutenant-General Sir George Luck, commanding in Bengal. It is notified that the 3rd Battalion South Lanca- shire Regiment, which went to South Africa a few months ago, is now located at Colesberg in the Northerk portion of Cape Colony. Lieutenant Woodham, just promoted to a com- mission in the Liverpool Regiment. from Qaarter- master Sergeant has been apoointed Quartermaster of the 2nd Battalion Liverpool Regiment, in place of Lieutenant Bell who has been placed on half pay owing to ill-health. Lieutenant Bell, who has been for a considerable period in the ranks of the Liver- pool Regiment, at one time served with the Depot Companies at Warrington. Second Lieut. Hay, of the sth battalion Liverpool Regiment, with headquarters at Warrington, has been given a commission as second Lieut. in the East Lancashire Regiment and joins the details of that corps now temporarily attaPhed to the 4-b battalion Liverpool Regiment. Mr. Hay joined the sth battalion Liverpool Regiment last January. Lieut. Whittle of the sth battalion Liverpool Regi- meat with headquarters at Warrington has a,sumel duty in Africa being appointed a subaltern in the Uganda Rifle,. Major Neville acts as second in command of the 2nd battalion Cheshire Regiment during the time Lieutenant Colonel Graham is employed on the staff lin South Africa. _ _ _ _

Captain Christian 3rd battalion Cheshire Regi- ment, and Captain Symes, WA battalion Manches- ter Regiment, have gone to West Africa for special service in connection with the Ashanti expedition. Caotain Hamilton has been promoted to the rank of Major in the Lancashire Fusiliers. He joined the regiment in October 1881 and was lately serving in Lancashire as adAtant of the 6tl battalion of the regiment.
