On Monday evening a little girl of about four years old, daughter of Mr H Selby, Earlestown, had a remarkably narrow escape from serious injury in Earle-street. She was crossing:: the street just as a milk float and horse driven by a lad was coming down. The horse was going at a good rate, and before the child cou!d get out of the way it was knocked down by the horse, aria the float passed over it, one of the wheels just grazing its head. When picked up it was found to have escaped with a shaking and a f-w bruises" GAMING AT BIIRTONWOOD.

INEWTJN CONGREGATIONAL MUTUAL IMPROVE-

MENT SOCIETY.

A wagonette excursion was undertaken on Sa:lur- day laQt, to Buckiow Hill, near Knutsford, by the members and friends of the Young Hen's Congre- gational Mutual Improvement Society, Crow-lane. The drive which occupied about two hours each way' was greatly enjoyed, though the sky during most of the afternoon was cloudy. Tea—as per arrangement—was provided at the "Clarion House," after which most of the party strolled as far as Rostherne Mere, seizing the opportunity also Of a visit to the Old. Parish Church. The number of cyclists and others who visit the district may be taken as evidence of the attractiveness and natural beauty of the place. The route going,after leaving Warrington was through Latcriford and High Legh whilst the return journey was by Lymm and Thel- well Though the pleasantness of the outing was interfered with slightly by an occasional shower, the trip was greatly enjoyed, the party arriviu,,z back again at the schoolroom about 10.30.

He said it was res-rvad for the beginning of the seventh century to usher into the Caurch the public worship of the 2aertid Heart of Jesus, which spread like a conflagration throughout the Catholic world. I A FLYING VISIT TO DOUGLAS. With a Peep at Port Erin. [By "QuILP N.l We travel quickly in these days. The steamer and the locomotive are altering the perspective of things. What at one time appeared an interminable distance and almost impossible of access is now brought within easy react) of - all. Within the writer's recollection, a visit to the Isle of Man was looked upon as a very long and tedious, not to say hazardous, journey. But on Saturday over a thou- sand of our townspeople accomplisned the journey thither, spent nine hours on the beautiful island, and returned safely to their homes in Warrington within 24 hours from leaving them. Such an excur- sion was not even dreamt of a few years ago, and it it were possible to tap on the shoulder one of our great-grandfathers find tell him of such a rapid flight tie would indeed think that we had become posssssed of wings. More surprised still would he be to find that the persons who undertook the journey were chiefly engaged in the manufacture of Richmond's gas stoves, and we can almost imagine him telling us with a sigh that such things were unknown in the" goou old days." , Of course such a journey as that from Warring- ton to Douglas and back in a single day is not an unalloyed pleasure. It imposes a strain upon the physical resources, and in some cases the tripper comes home the worse rather than the better for the outing. To be on pleasure bent for twenty hours at a time is no mean effort and can only be thoroughly understood by those who have experi- enced it. The tripper, however, is a cheery in- dividual, and it matters little to him how weary he may be at the close of the day. Like many another pereoa in Warrington I was up at an early hour on Saturday in order to be at the Central Station by five a.m, It is one of the characteristics of the day tripper to be up with the lark, and it is perhaps questionable whetner that feathered songster was up as early as 801319 of the trippers who left Warrington on Saturday morn- ing: One of toe first things I did on awakening at the unusual hour of 4 a m. was to take stock of the westher. It was then fine but the clouds looked very threatening for rain and the general outlook was not at all pcomising for a pleasant day. Des- pite the unpromising state of affairs, I ventured forth and found e that there were many wending their way to the railway stations. On the road I meta woman faithfully discnarging her duties as a "knocker-up" by gently tapping the windows with a small bundle of wire attacned to a long pole. She is a familiarfigure to those whose duty it is to be out early in the morning, and for many years she has eked out a living by arousing a younger, genera- tion to their daily labour. On nearing the Central Station, the stillness of the morning air was broken by the sound of music proceeding from the Borough Baud whin had been specially engaged to accom- pany Messrs. Richmond's excursion to Douglas. With an enterprise characteristic of this firm' the splendid steamer "rhe Priace of Wales" had been Specially chartered to take their employei and friends to the Isle of Man. I was one of the thou- sand and odd persons who availed themselves of the opportunity. To convey this large Company to Liverpool three trains were requisitioned and it may be readily imagined that the scene at the Cen- tral Railway station was an animated one. When the first train made its appearance alongside the plattorms the excursionists did not exactly fight for seats in the carriages, but tovut it mildly, there wds a rush for them. Nothing untoward happened and those who did not succeed in journeying by the tint train tied the satisfaction of knowing that 1 there was plenty of room in the trains which were to follow immediately afterwards. True to the reputation of the C.L.C. Railway Com- pany, the trains were got off with punctuality and by half-past five the excursionists were hurrying through the streets of Liverpool to the landing stage. ' The city was just awakening to the work of the day. By electric tram and overhead railway, workmen with their breakfast cans and food in their hands were proceeding to the docks and other places of business. On the other hand, workmen who had been repairing the streets during the night time were just finishing work. On reaching the landing stage, a freshening breeze from the river gave the visitor a foretaste of what was to follow. At the far end of the stage lay the "Prince of Wales" gaily bedecked with bunting. Already many of the excursionists had boarded her and were rushing about with band-boxes and deck-chairs eager to get the best seats. Not even a stranger could have mistaken that it was Richmond's excur- sion. There were " Kruger" posters advertising Richmond's specialities at almost every turn. It was a merry throng that assembled on the boat,and in case the Liverpudliens had never heard the strains of " Soldiers of the Queen," the bend gave them the benefit of it as a parting gift. Not to be out-done the Ragged School Band which was also present, playen one of their familiar tunes. It was to music such as this that the steamer entered upon its journey to Mona's Isle. The genial weather- beaten captain piloted the vs-sel through thn channel and the excursionists who had not gone down to the cabins enjoyed the sights which the river possesses in a marked degree. As we steamed past the ocean greyhound—the Campania—we were , able to admire her ample proportions an l contrast , her size with that of the vessel on which we were travelling. New Brighton and its Tower came in for a good deal of notice and comment, bur, shortty after passing it the weateer took an unfavourable turn and drizzling rain was ex- perienced for a brief period. The angry sea also began to assert itself with unpleasant results. Its first overt act was to baptise a number of passengers in the bow of the vessel with a spray of sea water. This was greeted with a hearty laugh,and as though designed to keep up the merriment, the first spray was followed by another and still another until at last the passengers were obliged to beat a hasty re- treat. Some ardent young spirits endeavourtA to maintain their position but they were drenched for doing tumuli at last this pert of the deck was cleared with the noticeable exception of two men who had chimed to the top of a poster erection, and whose courage in \ sticking to their position was much admired. 'tithe meantime a change had also come over many of the passeneers. The rolling and pitch- ing of the vessel were effecting them unpleas way. Groups of them were sitting and lying about suffer- ing from sea-sickness. At no time do people look and feel more miserable, and as one gazed en the suffering trippers on Saturday, there seemed to be some truth in the statement that we take our pleasures sadly. Here was to be seen for instance a Ragged School lad with his flute lying carelessly by his side, sick and sadly, looking as if he would be thankful for some- one to throw him overhoard. Near to, sat helpless men and women, some with their heads resting on their hands and others holding cups of tea which they did not want to taste,Bll suffering silently and wishing they were on shore again. They were to be pitied and yet there were few to pity. There were even some who were ready to deride them. One man in particular thought it was a fine thing ' to laugh at, but there was a laugh on the other side when a littls later he was crying for aSsiStatlee in his sickness. We had two bands aboard but there was no music! Silence is golden ! Four hours' sailing brought us to the breakwater at Douglas and short as the sail was it was far too long for many. It was with feelings of relief that the passengers again set foot on terra-firma. Many Warrington people had assembled on the pier head , to bid us welcome and as one walked along the Loch promenade encircling the beautiful crescent-shapsd bay, with its backeround of terraced cliffs and splendid baildings bathed in the warm sunlieht,the visitor was able to understand somewhat the attrac- tiveness of Dougles. My stay in Douglas was a very brief one. Immediately after arriving I ascended a char-s-bane standing oppeedte the Villiers Hotel where Mr. Richmond an i his staff assembled. and afterwards started fora drive across the island to Port, Erin. It was a delightful tour through a country where natuee looks at her best Honeysuckle and fuschias grew wild on the way- side and trim little fsrmsteads dotted the landscape here and there Smiling youngsters turned somer- saults and performed other gyrations with shouts of" Hi-Kelly," and were the recipients of coopers. An ascent of a few hundred feet was rewarded by a fine view of the cliPtarit Bes, which was rarely lost sight of on the journey. Sometimes you rode up a stiff incline and at other times you walked and derived pleasure from doing so. During the twenty miles journey two stoppages wer made for the purpose, AO it was said, of watering the horses. But in an instant the cars were empty, and water of a more or less diluted kind was naked for and supplied to the thirsty mortals wbo quite appreciated the necessity for a brief stoppage. I had always been under the • impression that Menx cats were without tails, but lo! here was one to be seen with a tail of which any cat might be proud.

It must surely have been an imported one—the cat, of course, I mean.

A delightful drive of three hours brought us to Port Erin, a quiet secluded spat, with a charming little bay, all sheltered by hills which run to the water's edge. It was one of the least likeliest places in the world to find a Warringtonian and yet here several were to be found epenaing a quiet holiday. Two of them, at least, Were being initiated into the mysteries of, golf, and seemed to be eujoy- ing themselves immensely. Dinner was served at the Belle Vue hotel where the party were the guests of Mr. Richmond. An excellent repast was partaken of for which the drive bad appreciably whetted the appetite. The stay in Port Erin was necessarily a brief one but the party went away with a somewhat pleasant recollection of the place. It was intended to make the return journey by char-a-banes but as the" gentle rain from heaven " began to descend it was decided to proceed to Douglas by train. The railways in the Isle of Man are indeed things to be remembered. They remind you of tram-lines and the carriages are slight improvements upon cattle trucks. You climb into them from the ground, platforms being evidently a superfluity in Manxlaud. When you get inside the carriage you find it divided into compartments by low-backed seats which enable you to sea from one end of the coach to the ocher. In the one in which I travelled the centre portion was labelled "smoking." A gentlema I occupying the next com- partment commenced smckieg and on being told that it was not a smoking compartment, simply put his head over the back seat of the centre portion,and thus complied with the regulations ! The railway journey of sixteen miles is completed in an hour if you are fortunate and not delayed by lack of steam or the late arrival of the mail bag at any of the stations. It is rumoured that the train once turned back to Port Sc. Mary for a bag of letters. When I returned to Douglas towards five o'elock a great, chars had come over tne scene. It was difficult to believe that it was the same place. The town that looked so bright and attractive in the morning was now a dull uninviting place drenched in rain. Tee streets were almost deserted and on the promenade a man was to be seen taking down the bedraggled flags which fluttered so gaily all round the bay in the morning sun. And where had the excursionists got to? They were glad to find shelter in refreshment rooms, liotels,and even on the boat which was in a few hours to take them home. Oh rain why were you so unkind as to cause a de- lightful day's excanioa to fizzle out in such a re- grettable way ? Some hard:things were said about the weather, but an excursionist is one who realises the necessity of making the best of things, and even rein does not prevent him from enjoying him- self. Oa the homeward journey when things were as bad as they could well nieh be, opportunity was taken to lance alemit the deck to the accompani- ment oi the band and sing popular airs. This was not altogether pleasant for those lying down under- neath and about the deck where mal de-mer was sadly too much in evidence, but it was taken in good part and all were glad when the Mersey WAS entered once more. In the murky distance could he seen the gorgeous illuminations at the New Brighton Towe.r which looked like a fairy scene be- side the shimmeriug water, Immediately after the vessel passed the Tower, the lights were turned out and served to show the surrounding darkness. Wet and in some cases miserable, the passengers arrived in Liverpool and there were some who vowed never to go to the Isle of Man egaio. Tney may now have changed their minis. I hope so.

ST. JOHN'S CHITACH UHOIR.

The annual picnic in coArisction- with this choir took place on Saturday. Upwards of 60 members and friends including the Rev J C M.atthews. and Mr J Harrison, organist, left Earlestown at 7-25 for Morecambe, where they arriviicl about 10-45. Be • fore dialler, a number took the opportunity of visiting the Artillery Camp. An excellent dinner was served and partaken of at the star Hotel end thoroughly enjoyed. After dinner char-a-bancs were engaged for a drive to Lancaster, returning to Morecambe ab)ut 3 o'clock. After a visit to the pier, tea was served at the Star Hotel and the Win- ter Gardens were aftsrwards visited. A start was made for horns about 7 o'clock, arriving at Bayles- town about 9.45. The trip was fully enjoyed by all, and was vored the best the choir had had.

Gospel Hall. On Sunday the preacher was Mr. A. Lorimer of Man- chester using the 14th chap. of 11. Samuel and Korn. 319 26' as the subject. Toe story of Absalom's return to his father David alter murdering his brother Amnon. The problem which David could not solve of how to bring back his beloved son and yet satisfy justice was saved by/God in Romans iii. David's heart yearned after his son but as a king he must mete out justice without partiality and justice for Absalom meant death. For four or five years he tried but could no: knd at last affection got the best of his ju igrnen: an i Absalom was restored only to turn out a rebel and a curse. God to-day seeks the wanderer and can save such because Jesus satisfied all the claims of justice.
